GUILTY. Aged 29.— Strongly recommended to mercy by the Jury. .— Confined Seven Days
Before Mr. Baron Platt.
[trialAccount t18470920-2217]
2217. JOHN HUTCHINGS was indicted for the willful murder of Mary Hutchings:—He was also charged on the Coroner's Inquisition with the like murder.
MESSRS. BODKIN and HUDDLESTON conducted the Prosecution.
MERCY LYDIA BYWATER. I am the wife of William Bywater, of St. Paul's Deptford—I knew the deceased for seven years—her name was Mary Hutchings—she was the prisoner's wife—I lived next door to them. On Sunday evening the 22nd of Aug., I was called into their house, about eight o'clock, or it might be a little before—I directly went up stairs, and found the deceased down by the side of the bed—she was dressed—she was very bad indeed, and complained of pain at the bottom of her stomach—she was very sick—I undressed her, and got her into bed—the prisoner was at home, their two sons and the youngest daughter, that is all the family that are at home—the deceased said she was very poorly, and I went down stairs to the prisoner, to get some brandy for her—I got some from him, he mixed it in hot water, brought it up stairs to me, and I gave it to the deceased—she drank it, and threw it up immediately—the prisoner had sent out the boy for the brandy—I had no conversation with him while the boy was gone for it—when I first went into the house, he said to me, "Mrs. Bywater, I wish you would go up to my wife; she is very bad, and she has been taking some d—stuff or other" that was before I went up stairs.
COURT. Q. Did you tell deceased what her husband had said? A. No—I asked her what she had been taking, and she said, "Only a little jalap"—when I came down stairs, I told the prisoner that his wife said she had taken nothing else only a little jalap, and he said that it was a d—d lie, he knew that she had taken something else—he said it was bitter orange, he could not recollect exactly—and next morning he said it was bitter aloes.
Q. When you came down and said she had taken something else, did he then tell you what that something else was? A. Either bitter orange, or bitter aloes—he told me that at that time, when I came down.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. I believe you asked him to make some tea? A. Yes—he did so, and he told me that he had made some before, in the after-noon—I gave the tea to the deceased, she threw it up directly—I also gave her some beer, which she threw up—I came down stairs, and said, "Mr. Hutchings, you must send for a doctor, and you must have some brandy for Mrs. Hutchings, she is very bad"—the eldest son at home went for Dr. Downing, who came about nine o'clock or half-past—I got the assistance of Mrs. Moulton, a neighbour, to attend on the deceased—she continued getting worse, and died about half-past one on the Monday morning.
Q. What sort of a woman was Mary Hutchings? A. She was a very nice woman, and one might be very comfortable with her—the prisoner and her lived on very bad terms—I had no conversation with the prisoner before his wife's death, as to the state in which she was—I do not remember it—I told him once that Dr. Downing said she was in a dangerous state.
Q. Did he say anything to you about arsenic? A. He said he had some arsenic in the drawer—that was said in the morning, after the corpse was laid out—he might have mentioned it to me before—I cannot say exactly, but it was mentioned when we were all sitting down to have a cup of tea, after the corpse was laid out—he said in the first place that he broke the drawer open, took the bottle out of the drawer, and emptied the contents into the fire—he then said he threw the bottle into the garden; then he said he threw it into the field, and after that he said he gave it to the boy to threw down the privy.
COURT. Q. Did he say when he had broken open the drawer? A. About half-past five in the evening, as near as I can tell—he said he had broken open the drawer about half-past five in the evening—he mentioned it on the Sunday evening as well.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. You are giving a very confused account, did he say anything on the Sunday evening about the arsenic? A. Yes—he said in my presence that he had broken open the drawer, and taken the arsenic away, and that these was no more in the bottle than you could lay on a sixpence, or his thumb-nail, and that he had thrown the contents into the fire and given the boy the bottle to throw down the privy—I cannot say at what time on Sunday evening it was that he said that—it was in the course of the time that I went up and down stairs—it was before the deceased's death—I do not know how long before—it was not the tea was prepared—I was not sent for till eight o'clock in the evening—if you will give me a minute's time to consider I will tell you—I think, as near as I can judge, it was about ten o'clock that he mentioned about the arsenic—that was after Dr. Downing had been.
COURT. Q. When he mentioned this at ten o'clock on Sunday night, did he mention at what time he had so broken open the drawer? A. Half-past five.
Q. Did he state at that time why he broke open the drawer? A. Because he was afraid his wife had got to it; that he threw it away in case she had got to the arsenic—he did not know whether she had got to it, or whether she had not, but he threw it away for fear she should get to it, as she was so bad—he did not say, in case she had got to it, but in case she should get to it—I might have said "in case she had got to it"—I do not know that I did.
Q. Do recollect yourself, it is exceedingly important, perhaps on the breath of your mouth hangs the life of this man? A. He said that in case she had got to it that he had made away with it—in case she should get to it, I mean—he did communicate to me a fear that she had got to it, and he threw it away to prevent her getting to it, and yet he said he knew she could not have got to it, for the boy had got the key of the drawer, and yet he was afraid she had, through her being so had.
Q. Did he say that all at once? A. One after the other.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Was this said on the Sunday evening? A. Yes, and on the Monday, towards the evening, he said there was no more in the bottle than you might lay on a sixpence, or the top of his thumb-nail—then he said he had thrown the bottle into the garden; then he said he threw it into the field, and then he said down the privy—the daughter-in-law made answer and said, "Which did you do father, why don't you say one thing?"—and then he said, "I gave the bottle to the boy to throw down the privy.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Were you the constant companion of the unfortunate woman? A. I was, we were great friends—that was known to her husband—when I came in and found her in this state of pain I was of course a good deal excited—I got a bottle of hot water to put to her stomach—I had a young baby with me, and I put it in bed by her side, and she wished me to come to bed with her—I had been with her before when she was poorly—she seemed very anxious for me to do everything I could for her—I went into my own house and fetched the hot water for her stomach.
Q. Do you happen to remember who it was that first mentioned about arsenic? A. Mr. Hutchings himself—the first time he mentioned it I do not think any one was present but myself—on the following morning, when it was talked of, Mrs. Moulton was present, and a person who we called in to lay the deceased out—it was after her death, proposals were made for me to go to Mr. Downing for the certificate.
COURT. Q. Did you undress her? A. Yes—she had a pocket—I did not see the pocket till after her death—I did not see the contents of her pocket, only as the prisoner took out of the pocket the money that he had given her before.
Prisoner. I did not touch it—the pocket was brought down stairs and given to me. Witness. I did not touch it myself—it was brought down stairs and given to him.
COURT. Q. My inquiry has reference to a key, not to any property? A. I did not see any key—I was a very particular friend of hers for seven years—I never saw any drawer or any place that she herself locked up—there might have been—I never saw her lock any up—what I had the use of after her death was all left open, not locked—I opened them—they were not locked—I do not think there were any locks to the cupboards—I believe there were locks to the drawers—I cannot tell whether she had any key to those locks—I did not look inside her pocket—I undressed her, but I did not think of
looking into her pocket for keys—I did not go to the drawer that was said to have been broken open, because the husband and son had nailed the lock on again—I saw the outside of the drawer—I did not see any other drawer there locked up—that was the drawer that belonged to the boy—it was one of a chest of drawers—all the drawers were not locked—I cannot say whether some of them were—I did not try them all—I tried some, and those that I tried were all open—they had locked on—the deceased used to place some clothes in those drawers that were in her son's room, as well as in other part of the house.
Q. Who sent for you to come to her? A. The prisoner, by his little boy—I did not hear him say he was glad I had come—he wished me to go up to his wife to assist and help her—I went to do that.
Q. Do you recollect, at any time before she died, her stating to you that she expected she should die? A. She used sometimes to say to me that she thought she should die a very bad death; not during this illness—she expected to get well when she got rid of her pain—she had not the least doubt of it, for she even said to me and the person with me, "We will have a cup of tea together about two o'clock"—I do not think she made any observation to her husband when he came up with the brandy—she was very thankful for everything she had—I do not know that he said anything—he only said, "There is the brandy"—she said nothing to him then—she had a fit, and I went down stairs directly, and told him there was something bad, that I was afraid there was something broken—he came up, and said, "Dear Mary, how do you fell now?"—she made answer, "Oh, I am not any worse," and he directly went into the next room and called his eldest son, that was at home, to come and see his mother—he then went down stairs, and knocked for his eldest son, that lived a door or two away, for him to come and see his mother as quick as possible—the deceased complained to me of a pain in the lower part of her belly—she said something to me—the prisoner was not by at the time—when I went to her she was lying in her own room, not in the room where the chest of drawers were—they were in the son's room.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Did you hear any noise from their room the night before? A. No. I did not.
MARY MOULTON. I am the wife of William Moulton, of St. Paul's Deptford. I was called in by Mrs. Bywater to the house of the deceased before her death—after her death, we went down to have a cup of tea, and whilst we were sitting having it, the prisoner said that Mrs. Hutchings was down stairs to dinner on the Sunday, and after dinner be went to lie down, and after being up stairs a little while he heard her groan—that he asked her what was the matter, and she said, "Oh, nothing particular"—he said he was up stairs, that he laid a little while longer, and he got up to see about his youngest son—that he heard her groan again, and he said, "What is the matter with you? what have you been taking?"—she said, "Only a little jalap"—he said, "That be d—d, you have been taking something else, I know"—he did not say what—directly afterwards, he said, "I had some arsenic in the house; and I took and broke open the drawer and made off with it"—I had known the deceased for some time, not to be particularly acquainted with her—her husband and her did not live happily.
COURT. Q. Were you long in her room before she died? A. I was with her about two hours before she died—she died almost on my arm—I do not know whether she had any keys—there was nothings of that sort found in her pocket—her little girl used to come to school to me, and we used frequently
to stand and speak at the door, but I was not in the habit of going to sit and chat with her—she had three fits before she died—I remember the prisoner coming up and addressing her as "Dear Mary"—he took hold of her hand, and said, "Mary, my dear, how are you now?"
Q. As far as you could judge, did her appear to be sincere in making that inquiry after her health? A. I cannot say anything as to that—I did not notice that she withdrew her hand from him—I do not know that I should have noticed it if she had done so, as I was on the other side of the bed—he merely spoke, and went down again—her reply was, "I am a little better," or "I am no worse"—I cannot say exactly what time this was—I think it was about eleven o'clock at night—we were down stairs at breakfast by two o'clock—the doctor had been then—Mrs. Bywater saw him—some medicine was sent to relieve her of her pain—I was not present when it was given—Mrs. Bywater had given it to her before I went in—she did not complain of any pain particularly at the time I was there—she was not in any rack of pain but what she could bear it—she complained to me of pain in her inside.
THOMAS HUTCHINGS. I am the prisoner's son—I was sixteen year old last Aug.—I lived at home with my father and mother—about two months ago my father got some arsenic from an ostler named Wheeler, for the purpose of killing the cats which came into the back yard, and ate my young chickens—the arsenic was in a phial—it was not mixed in anything—it was a powder—after it was brought home, some of it was mixed for the purpose of putting in the garden to kill the cats—my father helped to mix it—I cannot say whether any of the cats were killed by it—I found no dead cats in out garden—my father put it out for them, I did not—I have seen him put it out—it was kept in my drawer, locked up, in the back chamber up stairs—I had the key of it—it was not in the room where my father and mother slept—it was in the room where I and my little brother slept—I have a sister younger than my brother—she slept along with my father and mother, in the front room, on the same floor as ours—I think I had seen the arsenic on the Sunday before my mother's death, not after that—that was seven days before—I had not opened the drawer for seven days before—I did not then go to it for the purpose of getting the arsenic—I saw it was then safe, and I looked the drawer—on the Sunday on which my mother died I had the key of that drawer in my pocket all day—I have it now ( producing two keys)—the small key is the key of the drawer—the other is a key that was picked up and given to me, it does not belong to anything—I do not think my mother knew about the arsenic being there—I never mentioned the arsenic in her presence, that I know of—I had no reason for not letting her know it—the bottle was not half full when I last saw it—on the Sunday morning in question I got up to have my breakfast—I had it in the usual way—I do not know who got it ready—my mother came down to breakfast—I do not know whether my father was down first—I do not know whether I went out after breakfast or not—I did not go out and come back at dinner time—I dined at home that Sunday, when I came home—I do not think I went out that Sunday morning—we dined about one o'clock—I think I was at home the whole of the day till dinner time—I took the dinner to the baker's—I think I came back directly—I did not go to church—my mother was down at dinner—we had a baked pudding, baked potatoes, and a piece of beef, I think—my mother ate some dinner—she seemed to be not very well, and did not eat much dinner—about half an hour after dinner she was sick and vomited—I went out a little after two o'clock, soon after I had seen my mother sick—she was then down stairs in the front room—my
father went up stairs to lie down, as he mostly did of a Sunday afternoon—he went up immediately after dinner—I came home again at past five o'clock—tea was nearly ready then—my father was then down stairs—I think my younger brother was there—they were getting the tea ready—my mother was up stairs—I went up to see her before tea—she was lying on the bed in my room, the same room in which the drawer was—I think she had her clothes on—she was groaning—she did not appear to be much worse than when I went out that I could see—I do not recollect that my father said anything to me before I went up to see my mother.
Q. Try and recollect, did he say anything about the drawer? A. Yes, he told me he had broken my drawer open, and taken the bottle out, and emptied the stuff into the fire—that was down stairs as soon as I came in, before I went up to see my mother—I do not recollect that he said what he had done with the bottle—I will tell the truth—I cannot recollect that he said anything about the bottle.
Q. Did he give you any reason for having broken open the drawer and emptied the stuff into the fire? A. Yes, because my mother had told him she had taken jalap, and he said he thought she had taken something more than jalap—that was all he said about it—it was then that I went up stair and found my mother groaning—I found that my drawer was taken out, and placed on a box—the lock was broken, as if it had been forced, and the bottle was gone—the drawer was lying a very short distance from the bed where my mother was—I do not recollect that my mother spoke to me when I went up—I said nothing to her then about the drawer or the bottle—I did when I went up a second time—the first time I went up I put the lock on the drawer again—my father came and helped me to nail it on—her went up with me—I got some nails, put the lock on again, and put the drawer in its place—my father helped me to do it—I heard nothing pass between my father and mother on that occasion—there were no other bottles or any medicine in that drawer—I think my father came down out of the room with me after meading the drawer, I am not certain—I went up a second time, after I had my tea—after putting on the lock, I went down and had my tea—me father was there while I had my tea, as well as I can recollect—I think there was ten poured out for my mother—I cannot say who took it up to her—I do not think I did—some tea was taken up by somebody—I saw it taken up, but cannot tell who took it—I cannot tell whether it was my father or my little brother—I cannot say who poured out the tea for her, I did not—it was about six o'clock that we had tea—my mother was ill at dinner time—she had been well enough in the morning to get the dinner ready and make the pudding.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know anything about your mother having taken any jalap? A. No, I know nothing about it either one way or the other—the pudding we had was plain pudding, under the meat—I had taken it to the baker's and brought it home—we also had a cold mulberry and applepie, baked on Saturday—it came up whole on the Sunday, it had not been cut—I kept books, pictures, and things of that sort in the drawer where the arsenic was—I was in the habit of keeping the key in my trowser's pocket—I got up that morning a little before nine—on other morning I get up at five o'clock—my mother knew that I had this drawer locked, and that I always had the key with me—I had on the same trowsers that I have now, on that Sunday morning—I have a pair of better ones at home—I do not remember ever leaving the key in my other trowssers I had on my best trowsers that Sunday—I have bought these since—I
wore the same trowsers on the Sunday that I had worn on the Saturday—I think my mother was up before me on the Sunday morning—I do not remember whether or not she came into my room—she used not to come into my room to call me of a morning—she used to call me at the foot of the stairs—I do not think she called me that morning—my little brother is eight years old.
Q. Where was this arsenic set to kill the cats? A. In the back garden, on the tiles—it was not done in the day time, but at night—I used to set it at night—I went down with my father to set it—I have seen him mix it up in a bit of grease in a bit of an oyster-shall—I have seen him take it out of the bottle for that purpose—he shook it out into the oyster-shell—I have not known the oyster-shell to be left about, before it was set in the garden.
Q. Did he borrow your key for that, or used you to open the drawer for him? A. I used to open the drawer—my mother was in the house at the time—she was not much out—I do not know whether my little brother knew that I had it or not—I had chickens which the cats used to kill—I work at Mr. Emery's, the cooper—I am not an apprentice there—I get regular wages, 7 s. a week—my father is a cooper—he did not become very ill some time ago—I do not know that he was ruptured—he was in regular work at this time—I put the dinner on the table on the Sunday—I cannot say whether my mother was sitting down at the table at the time or not—I saw her make the pudding between ten and eleven o'clock—she made it in the back room down stairs—I am quite sure that the arsenic was never poured out into anything but an oyster-shell, or a bit of pantile, or something of that kind—I never used a saucer or cup—I have never lent my father the key of the drawer—the arsenic was not poured out in the room, but in the back yard—the bottle used, to be taken out into the back yard, as far as I know, and there it was used, and then it was taken back again and put into the drawer—the bottle was. I think, about half full when I first had it—I knew it was poison, and that it was dangerous, and was careful about it—the word "poison" was written on a paper that was on the bottle—I cannot read and write—I cannot read writing—I had a Testament and another book in my drawer—I have read the Testament sometimes—my father did not buy it me, it was my grandfather's, and I had it—I never knew of my mother taking much medicine.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Could you read the word "poison" on the bottle? A. No—I know that it was "poison" that was on the paper—I cannot say whether it was printed—it was on a piece of paper tied with a string round the neck of the bottle—I could read the word "poison" on it—I cannot tell how many times I have had it mixed in the garden to kill the cats—I do not think it was as many as six—it might have been three or four times—I cannot recollect the last time I mixed it—I had not done so on the Saturday before my mother died, nor for weeks before—I had on the same trowsers on the Sunday till dinner time that I had had on the Saturday—I then changed them—that was not after my father had emptied the stuff—it was just at dinner-time—after dinner—when my father told me that he had broken open the drawer, I felt in my pocket to see whether I had the key—I am quite sure I had it then in my pocket—those were my best trowsers, that I had put on after dinner.
COURT. Q. Your habit was to rise early in the morning on a week-day? A. Yes—I usually got home a little before eight o'clock at night—I generally went to bed about ten, or a little after—I and my little brother mostly went to
bed before my father and mother, leaving them up, below stairs—I always took off my clothes when I went to bed—I do not lay awake much at night—I used to sleep very sound—I have seen the cats come into the garden and run away with my chickens—I have mentioned before my mother that the cats used to kill my chickens, and I used to say that I would do something with them that they should not have my chickens—I never told her what I had got to prevent them having my chickens—when the poison was to be mixed I used to go up stairs myself for the bottle—my father was below stairs—he would send me up when my mother was there—there was no secrecy about it at all—my mother must have heard my father give me directions to go up and get the bottle—I cannot recollect what he used to tell me to go up for—I do not know whether there were any other drawers in the house locked up except mine—I dare say there were—my key was the only one in the house that would fir the drawer—I never tried it to see whether it would fit any other drawer—I have never seen my father or mother with any key—I do not know whether anything else was locked up in the house besides my drawer—I never went to any other drawer—I cannot say whether my father had any drawer that he locked up—I dare say he had—my mother had some boxes—I think she had keys to them—I never went to them—I think my mother knew that the arsenic was in the drawer—I am not sure—if she had asked me whether I had such a thing I should not have denied it, because I have said that I would get something for the cats—my mother was down stairs when I said that—my mother was crying after she made the pudding—my father was gone out then—I went with the pudding to the baker's—I cannot say how long I was gone with it—I do not think it was a quarter of an hour—when I came back I stayed in the house until I went to the barker's again—I was with my mother during that time, as far as I can recollect—I was in and out of the back yard and about—my mother was down stairs when I came back from the baker's the first time—I cannot tell what she was doing—I went for the dinner about one o'clock—we all ate the same thing—every one of us ate of the pie, the pudding, and the beef—mother did not have any pie—she did not eat much dinner—she did not seen very well, I thought—I do not think she ate as much as she usually did—about half an hour after dinner she was sick—I was in the house during that time—I cannot tell at what time my father went out that morning—I think he was out at the time she was crying—I am not sure—he did not come home before I took the pudding to the baker's—he came home while I was gone for the dinner—I think he went out before my mother began to make the pudding, but I am not sure—I went up twice to my mother at tea-time—I do not think my father was up both times with me—he went up the first time, and assisted in putting the drawer to rights—I do not think he was up the second time.
JURY. Q. Did your father ever keep any spirits or sugar in the house! A. We mostly had sugar in the house—it was kept in the sugar-basin—it was not in a closet, and was never locked up—I could take what I pleased—we only had one chest of drawers in the house—I think this key would unlock the whole of the drawers—I never tried to lift up the lids of the boxes—I did not go into my mother's room very often—I never tried the key, except to my own drawers—I had two drawers; one I kept my clothes in, and the other was a half-drawer—the same key unlocked both drawers.
COURT. Q. Were the drawers that you used, the two small ones at the top? A. One of them—the bottle was in that one—I did not use the other small drawer—it used to be kept for nails, or anything of that sort—I think
there were three large drawers—there used to be clothes put in them—I do not know whose clothes—there were many things put in that I did not know of—mine and my father's clothes were put in—I do not think any of my mother's clothes were put there—there were shirts of mine and my father's—my mother used to look after our shirts—the three large drawers had locks on them—I believe they all had locks, except one of the small ones where the nails were—I never knew any other drawer to be locked than the one I had the bottle in—I am sure they never were.
CHARLES HUTCHINGS. I am between eight and nine years old. I slept with my brother in the room next to my father and mother—I remember bearing my father come home on the Saturday night before my mother died—I do not know what o'clock it was—I do not know how long it was after I had gone to bed—my mother was in bed, she got up, went down, and let him in, and he came up stairs, and went to bed—I heard something take place between him and my mother after he came home—I cannot recollect what it was—my mother said, "You have begun your games already"—I do not recollect what my father said—I have given an account of what he stated, before to-day—no one has told me not to state it now—he spoke loud—he said, "Hold your tongue"—I heard my mother call him a villain, and my father hit her—she was sitting up in bed—I did not see her—I heard her get out of bed and go down stairs, and I heard the front door shut when she went out—she slammed to the door—I do not recollect whether my father called out anything after her as she was going out—I told the gentleman who examined me what he said, one Saturday night after my mother's death—I remember going before the Coroner at the Garrick's Head, and to Mr. Sandham's office, in High-street, Deptford—I told Mr. Sandham what my father called out to my mother as she went down stairs.
Q. Then, now tell us what you told him, if it was true? A. He called her, "Oh you faggot! "—that was when she went up stairs—my father slammed the door when he came in, and said, "Oh you faggot! "—he then went up stairs to bed—then he struck her, and then she got up, and as she went down stairs, he said, "You had better not come near me to-night"—I saw nothing more of my father and mother till next morning at breakfast time.
COURT. Q. You say she went down, did she go out of the house? A. Yes—I heard the door shut—she pulled it to, hard—she did not slam it—she pulled it to, hard, with the knocker—I did not hear my mother come in again—I went to sleep shortly after.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Was your brother asleep at this time? A. Yes in the same bed with me—after breakfast next morning I went to school—I came back at twelve o'clock—I cannot remember who let me in—my mother was out in the yard, cutting some cabbages for dinner—I afterwards went out, and fetched the dinner beer—my mother did not eat so much at dinner as she generally did—when dinner was over, my father went up stairs to lie down, and my mother went to lie down in the back room, not the room where we slept, but in the room where we had out dinner—I do not know what room my father laid down in—he went up stairs—soon after that, I went again to school—I came back at half-past four—my mother did not go up stairs after dinner—when I came back, my father and mother were up stairs—I did not go up stairs to see—I knocked at the door twice when I came home, nobody came, and I got over the place—I did not find anybody down stairs—I made the fire, to get the kettle to boil for tea—while I was doing so, my father came down stairs—I do not know whether my brother came home—I did not see him there at tea time—my mother stayed up stairs—my brother was not there
at the time the tea was made—I saw my brother again at night—there was nobody there at the time the tea was made but my father and myself—my father made the tea—at the time the tea was making, my father had a bottle—I threw that bottle down the privy—my father gave it to me, and told me to throw it down the privy—the bottle was empty when he gave it to me—I saw him take something out of it before he gave it to me—I do not know what it was—it was white stuff—I do not know whether it was white powder—there was white stuff in the bottle—he put that into the fire—as soon as he gave me the bottle, I took it to the privy, and threw it down—the tea was not poured out when I came back—the tea was made, there were the cups and saucers—when I came back, there was no tea in them—some tea was put is them after I came back—my father poured it out, and took some up stairs for my mother—I do not know whether he took up one cup, or more than one—I took some up afterwards—I do not know how soon it was after my father took up some tea that I took some up—it was at the same tea time—when I took it up, I found my mother in the room where my brother and I sleep—I said, "Father has sent you some tea," and put it down by her—she was lying on the bed—I do not know whether she appeared to be well or ill—she was lying on the bed with her clothes on—I afterwards went and fetched the cap and saucer away—my mother promised to give me something for attending to her, and spoke kindly to me—when I went up with the tea, I noticed that one of the small drawers was drawn out of the chest of drawers—I took the cup and saucer down stairs, and gave them to my father or put them down—he was down stairs—I did not notice whether my brother was there then—my brother Tom was not there—I do not know where Tom had his tea that day—when I came down, and put the cup and saucer down, my father gave me a halfpenny, and told me to go out and play—I then went out.
Cross-examined. Q. When you were examined by the gentleman at Deptford, were you promised anything, or told that you would be punished? A. No—it was about six o'clock on the Sunday evening when I saw my father making the tea—I had thrown the bottle down the privy before he made the tea.
COURT. Q. Before he put the water in the tea-pot? A. Yes—I saw him put the water in the tea-pot—I saw him put the water in the tea-pot after I took the bottle out to throw it into the privy—it was after I came back that I saw him take the tea out, and put it into the tea-pot—he did not put any water into the pot and wash it out first—he took the grouts out and chucked them away—he gave me the grouts to chuck away—he gave them to me in the tea-pot—I emptied the grouts out of the tea-pot and brought it him back again—that was after throwing the bottle away—I do not know how many tea-cups there were on the table—I had some tea out of the same tea-pot—there was sugar and milk in it—the tea-cups were kept on the shelf—I got them down, after I had thrown the bottle away—I am quite sure that I took up a cup of tea to my mother, and that my father did also—I am quite sure my father did—I saw the tea poured out into the cup that he took up, and the sugar and milk put to it—I cannot recollect whether we had anything to eat at tea—my father did not take my mother anything to eat—I do not know how he carried the tea up—I saw him go up stairs with it—he took hold of the saucer—I do not know whether he held it with both hands, because it was so full—I do not know how long it was before he came down again—he waited till she had drank the tea, and then he brought down the empty cup—I do not know whether he used the cup afterwards for himself or not—I do not know whether he took any tea himself afterwards—I cannot recollect—I
drank some tea after he had come down—he took some tea with me—after I took the tea up to my mother, I sat sown to drink my own tea—I do not recollect whether my father sat down and drank tea with me—I do not know whether my father went without his tea that evening—I ate something with my tea—my father cut me some bread and butter—I do not know whether he cut any for himself—I went out directly after I had my tea—my father was sitting in the room where I was—he was sitting at the same table where the tea was—the cup that I took up to my mother I took off the table when it was full of tea and took it up to my mother—there were three cups on the table, one for me, one for father, and one for mother—I did not pour out any of the tea—two cups had been poured out when my father went up with the first cup, one for my mother, and one for me—when my father came down he sent me up with the second cup—my father generally drinks tea—I do not know how long I was sitting at the table at my own tea after I came down from my mother—when I went away, I left my father sitting at the table—there was a cup before him, and the tea-pot on the table—all the thing were on the table, and I left him sitting at the table.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did your father cut the bread and butter after he had come down from taking the tea up to your mother? A. Yes, that was for me.
JURY. Q. What was done with the empty cup which your father brought down? A. Put on the table—I cannot tell what was done with it before I took up another cup of tea for my mother.
COURT. Q. Do you recollect whether you took up the tea in the same cup that your father had taken the tea in to your mother; was the same cup filled up again? A. I do not know—I do not know whether he threw she dregs out of the cup that he brought down, before he put more tea into it.
THOMAS WHEELER. I am ostler to Messrs. Blackmans, the coopers, where the prisoner and his son worked. About three months before his wife's death, I gave the prisoner a bottle with some arsenic in it—I gave it him because the said the cats ran away with the boy's chickens—the father and the boy both told me so—the bottle was little better than half full of white arsenic.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know that arsenic was a thing to use for that purpose? A. I had used it for poisoning rats—I told the prisoner I had used it for poisoning rats—it was a small bottle—there was a label tied round the neck of the bottle with "Arsenic, poison," upon it.
WILLIAM COCKLE. I am one of the beadles of Deptford parish—I searched the prisoner's premises, and found five packets and one bottle, which I handed over to Mr. Heisch—the packets were alongside the fire-place, in a corner, not in a cupboard, but on the top of a shelf—they were tied up as they were when I gave them to Mr. Hiecsh—I never had them unfolded—I found the bottle on the shelf alongside the packets—I was not present when any bottle was found in the privy—the bottle I speak of was found on the shelf—I know nothing of any other.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you find any tea-cups and tea-pot? A. I searched them all on the 27th, but I never looked at them.
COURT. Q. What did you do with the paper packets? A. I took them to the hospital, and gave them to the medical gentleman—I delivered them to him exactly in the same condition in which I found them.
WILLIAM SPACEY. I am a constable, of Deptford—I searched the privy of the house where the prisoner lived, and I found a bottle—I gave it to Mr. Heisch in the same in which I found it—I assisted in taking the prisoner into custody on the Friday after the inquest, the 27th I think—I told
him what I took him for—he said he was innocent—he went quietly part of the way—he then called me a b—and would not go—when we came to St. Paul's Churchyard, he said he wanted to make water—I followed him close behind—he said, "Are you frightened?"—I said, "No"—he said, "You b—, you could not take me, nor two b—b—s likewise"—I got a City policeman, and we took him.
COURT. Q. Did he resist being taken, or run away? A. No—he walked back, and up with his fist to strike me, and said, "You b—, stand up"—I had my truncheon in my hand, and struck him, or else he would have got away form me—he put his hand in the attitude of striking, which made me strike—he went quietly with the assistance of two City policemen.
CHARLES HEISCH. I am a lecturer upon practical chemistry at St. Thomas's Hospital. I have examined these five packages, two of them contain jalap, one contains carbonate of magnesia, another some pills, and another some brown sugar—the pills appear to be composed of common aloes, commonly called bitter aloes—I also examined the bottle—it had no label round it—it had apparently contained some organic matter in a state of decomposition, but it was impossible to state what it was, it was so completely decomposed—this is not the bottle that was taken from the privy—the bottle taken from the privy contained a quantity of night soil and traces of arsenic—there was certainly enough to enable me to say that the bottle had contained white arsenic ( the bottle was here produce)—it has no label on it, nor had it when it was given to me—I was not present at the post-mortem examination—I received the stomach from Dr. Taylor—I submitted it to a chemical test, and found that it contained a considerable quantity of white arsenic—I was afterwards present when the kidneys were taken out—I submitted them to the same analysis, and they also contained arsenic—I succeeded in reproducing arsenic to the extent of about fifteen grains, some portion of which I have here—( producing several small tubes)—this is arsenic in the metallic condition in which it came from the contents of the stomach, and this is some in the state of white arsenic—at my last experiment I found this white arsenic in a pure state to adhere to one side of this tube—fifteen grains would be quite sufficient to cause death—this other tube contains fifteen grains of arsenic—it is a about the same in bulk as an ordinary pinch of snuff, that might he held between the fingers.
COURT. Q. Would tea hold it in solution? A. To a certain extent it would, but it would be more suspended in the fluid than actually in solution—it would be held in the tea for a short time, but being rather neavy it would soon settle to the bottom—it would settle, perhaps, in the course of three or four minutes.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Supposing a person to stir the tea, as is usual, before taking it, it would then be sufficiently buoyant to be taken in it? A. Yes, it might then be taken—arsenic is as nearly tasteless as possible—supposing fifteen grains of arsenic to be put in a cup of tea, sweetened with sugar, and milk in it, it would not be detected by the taste—I did not see the deceased during her life.
Cross-examined. Q. In extracting this amount from the kidneys and stomach, you allow, I suppose, a good deal for loss; there must have been a great deal more than that taken into the stomach? A. Yes—it would be difficult to say whether there was double—it would depend a good deal upon the evacuations that took place after the poison had been taken.
COURT. Q. From what do you infer the fact of more having been taken into the stomach? A. From the fact of her having vomited frequently.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you that you extracted the whole that there was in the stomach at the time? A. Yes—if anything is allowed for loss it would be but a few hundredths of a grain—the taking of arsenic produce a burning sensation in the throat—that comes on some little time afterwards—in some cases it will take half an hour or more, it is very uncertain—the operation of arsenic is extremely uncertain in point of time, varying from half an hour to four or five—the sickness usually begins with pains in the stomach, but that is also uncertain—sickness frequently comes on a very short time afterwards, but sometimes we find cases where sickness does not occur at all—the pain is principally in the stomach and back part of the throat—that would render a person indisposed to eat or drink—sometimes it causes considerable thirst—they feel disposed to take cold liquids, but indisposed to take their food—very probably a little eaten would bring on retching and sickness.
COURT. Q. In what period of time would the indisposition to eat commonly arise? A. It would be very difficult to say, it would depend so much upon the period at which the person began to eat before taking the arsenic—if it is taken upon a full stomach it does not act so speedily as if it is taken upon an empty stomach—if it was taken about an hour before dinner, probably at dinner time the person would not feel disposed to eat—that is the ordinary consequence of an exhibition of arsenic.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You have some powders there? A. Yes, they were brought to me by the beadle as having been found in the house—amongst them there is an aloetic pill—it is what is usually called bitter aloes—it contains bitter aloes—it is an aperient medicine—here is also some jalap—jalap is about as innocent a medicine as anybody can take—it sometimes slightly irritates the stomach—it not unfrequently causes a little nausea—it is a mild aperient—there is no direction upon these powders as to the amount to be taken—I could not tell whether was any jalap in the stomach—jalap is a vegetable—I did not apply any test to ascertain the existence of any vegetable matter in the stomach, and am not able to state whether there was any vegetable poison or medicine in the stomach at that time—if she had taken any large quantity of jalap in the course of the day upon which she died, I might possibly have found some traces of it in the stomach, but after so much vomiting it would be unlikely.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Would it be at all unlikely, a person's stomach being deranged, that jalap should cause vomiting? A. It would be extremely likely, it is a nauseous medicine—that vomiting, if caused by taking jalap or an aloetic medicine, would indispose the party to eat—a deranged stomach alone would indispose a party to eat—the immediate action of arsenic is by eating into the coats of the stomach—if the stomach is empty the arsenic is more quick in its operation—if it was taken an hour before dinner, upon an empty stomach, and caused nausea and vomiting, I should expect those symptoms to be accompanied by violent heat and pain in the region of the stomach—that would probably precede the vomiting.
Q. Then, suppose a person to have taken jalap, and to have been sick and indisposed to eat at dinner-time, and not to complain of any burning heat or sensation of pain at the stomach, would you think that the fact of the jalap having been taken, would account for that state of things? A. Certainly, more naturally than if arsenic had been taken in the mean time.
DR. CALEB TAYLOR. I was present at the examination of the body of the deceased, and took out the kidneys—I did not take out the stomach—it was brought to me by Mr. Downing, sealed up—he gave it me as the stomach of
the deceased, and I handed it to Mr. Heisch—I handed the kidneys to Mr. Heisch—I was not present when he instituted his chemical analysis.
EDWARD DOWNING. I am a surgeon. I was called to the deceased's home about nine o'clock on Saturday evening, the 22nd of Aug.—I found her up stairs—she complained of great thirst, constant vomiting, violent pain of the bowels, and involuntary diarrhoea—she did not complain of her throat—I felt her pulse, and found that the power of life was rapidly sinking—I asked her what she had taken, and I understood, I think, from Mrs. Bywater that she had taken some jalap—when I arrived at the house the prisoner sent the boy into the next-door neighbour, Mrs. Bywater—she acme in immediately, and went up stairs first, and I followed her—I did not learn from the deceased that she had taken anything—it was Mrs. Bywater, in her presence—I immediately gave her some brandy and water, and also requested some one to go to my house for some medicine, to lose no time; and I gave her some astringent medicine, as the diarrhoea was so severe—I considered from the symptoms, that it was an attack of cholera—I remained there some few minutes—I did not see her again—I was not sent for again—understand she died about one that morning—I saw the prisoner on the Sunday evening—I told him his wife was seriously ill, dangerously ill; and I would do all I possibly could for her—he did not make any reply—he said he would send for the medicine directly, and Mrs. Bywater said she would come for it.
Q. Did he give you any reason to believe that his wife had taken arsenie or any poison whatever? A. None whatever—he heard what I said—he said nothing at all to me about the situation of his wife—he was down stairs when I saw him—I only saw him once—he opened the door to me, I think, when I went in—I waited while he sent next door for Mrs. Bywater—I should think I was alone with him, altogether, seven or eight minutes—I then west up stairs with Mrs. Bywater—when I came down again I found him down stairs, and then I said that his wife was seriously ill, in great danger—he said nothing to me about her, on either occasion—I examined the body afterwards—I took out the stomach, and gave it to Dr. Taylor—Mrs. Bywater applied to me on the following morning for a certificate, which I refused—I was the first person that opened the body—I had no doubt then from the appearances that presented themselves, that her death was caused by poison—I have no doubt of it now—the stomach was highly inflamed, and also the duodenum—I did not examine the throat—there were appearances which justified me in taking out the stomach, and submitting it to a chemical analysis.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you ask the prisoner any question as to what his wife had taken in the course of the day? A. No, nor did I ask Mrs. Bywater—the woman herself was rapidly sinking, she was hardly able to articulate—I remained there seven or eight minutes, altogether—not less than that—I think it was seven minutes—I did not inquire what food she had taken that day.
THOMAS HUTCHINGS re-examined. I went out in the morning with the beef to the baker's, in my old trowsers—I changed my clothes before dinner—I do not think it was long before—I do not think it was above half as hour—I went up into my own room to change them—I did not go to the drawer in which I had the arsenic—I had an opportunity then of seeing the outside of the drawer—the drawers were all in their places at that time—I am quite sure of that—I observed nothing like a breakage of my small drawer—it was not out in the room—I came down after I had changed my clothes—my father did not go up stairs to my room afterwards, before we sat down
to dinner, that I saw; I never saw him go up—he was not down stairs when I was changing my clothes—he was out—he came home while I was gone for the dinner—I found him at home when I returned—I did not go into my room again till tea-time—we dined between one and two—I do not think I stopped in the house longer than half an hour after dinner—when I left the house after dinner, my father was gone up stairs to lie down—my mother did not go up too—I do not think my mother went up at all before I left the house—I cannot say exactly how long before I left the house, my father had gone up stairs; but I do not think more than a quarter of an hour.
GUILTY. Aged 43.— DEATH.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18470920-2218]
2218. JOHN PRITCHARD was indicted stealing 2 pewter pots, value 3 s. 4 d.; the goods of George Mason.
GEORGE MATTHEWS. I am servant to Mr. George Mason, a market-gardener and a publican; he keeps the Windmill, at Deptford. On the 1st of Sept. I saw two pots in a shed in his binding-ground—these pots produced are my master's property.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When had you seen them in this garden? A. At half-past seven in the evening—the garden adjoins the high road—I have seen the prisoner about three years before—he then used my master's house—I am foreman and salesman in the market-gardening business.
JOHN PAVOR ( policeman M 147.) I was on duty at half-past two o'clock in the night on the 1st. of Sept.—I came up to the prisoner, and asked what he had got under his jacket—he made no answer—I took him by the collar, put my hand under his jacket, and pulled out this quart pot—I put my hand under his other arm and pulled out this other—I asked whose they were—he said, "I believe they belong to Mr. George Mason, of the Windmill public-house"—he said he had picked them up under the hedge—I asked if he worked for him—he said, "No"—I asked how he came to take these pots, if he did not work for him—he then said he did work for him—I asked where he lived—he said, "In the brick-field," which is about a quarter of a mile from the spot—I said I must take him to the station—he became very violent—I threw him on the ground and made an alarm—my brother officer came, and we took him to the station.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say he was going to take them home, and take them to Mr. Mason next day? A. Yes, when I asked him, he said he was going to take care of them—he was not drunk—I could not say whether he had been drinking or not.
JURY to GEORGE MATTHEWS. Q. Were the post in the shed in the garden? A. Yes—the garden is enclosed by a hedge—the shed-door was shut—no one had any right there from the time I shut up the shed till the next morning.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 31.— Confined Three Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

these ladles—I asked him how he got then—he said he took them from Mr. Newhall's—I found a number of other things there.

2222. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing 3 yards of corduroy, value 3 s. 9 d.; the good of the Guardians of the Poor of St. George-the-Martyr, Southwark, his matters.
ELIZABETH SARAH O'BRIEN. I am matron of the workhouse of St. George-the-Martyr, Southwark, at the Lewisham—the prisoner was tailor there, at weekly wages—it was his business to cut out corduroy—he had no business to take any away—I have missed corduroy—I have seen some which is made into a pair of trowsers which Miller has on—I believe it is ours, but cannot swear to it—these other pieces I believe to be the same—they belong to the guardians of the poor of that parish.
CHARLSE MILLER. I am ostler at the Castle, at Lewisham—the prisoner made me this pair of corduroy trowsers which I have now on.
EDWIN BOURNE. The prisoner asked me to carry a parcel to Price from the workhouse-yard—it appeared to be cord or jean—I did so.
JOHN PRICE. I received these pieces of corduroy from the prisoner.
CHARLES LLOYD ( policeman, R 199.) I produce three pieces of corduroy, which I got from price.
Prisoner's Defence. I made two suits of clothes a week; I had two yards, or two yards and a half, according to the size of the lads; had I been short, I should have had to have made up the deficiency; I save pieces from the general cutting from one time to another.
JURY to ELIZABETH SARAH O'BRIEN. Q. was that the contract? A. No, it was not—I bought everything that I gave him—I gave him out sometimes five yards, and sometimes six yards, to make a suit of clothes—if he had any over he would use it for repairing the old suits—he had no right to keep any himself.
GUILTY. Aged 22.— confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18470920-2223]
2223. HEZEKIAH BURBAGE was indicted for unlawfully having in his possession certain naval stores, marked with the broad-arrow, the property of our Lady the Queen.
MR. PETERSDORF conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN WHITE ( policeman, R 180.) On the 19th of Aug. I was on duty in High-street, Deptford—I saw the prisoner driving a cart—I knew him before—I did not notice whether there was anything in it—he went with it to Hedges', a marine-store dealer, in High-street, Deptford, he got out of the cart, and went to a side gateway, attached to Mr. Hedge's shop—after remaining there about two minutes, two of Mr. Hedge's men came out of the yard, undid the tailboard, and took from the cart a basket covered with a cloth, and carried it into Mr. Hedge's yard (I did not see the prisoner at that time)—I followed into the yard, and saw the basket on the ground—the prisoner stood by it—I asked him what it contained—he said, "Copper"—I asked him to let me look at it—he said he would with the greatest pleasure—one of Hedge's men undid the cloth—I looked, and saw this copper in the basket—here are a great many pieces, weighing in all about 126lbs—I asked the prisoner how he accounted for it—he told me he bought it at different shop a Woolwich—I told him I was not satisfied, and should take him before a Magistrate, which I did—I examined the basket at the station, and found one piece with the broad-arrow on it—this is it—I knew the prisoner to be a marine-store dealer.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have any of the other copper articles the broad-arrow on them? A. No—I knew where the prisoner lived—he went to the station very readily—he has a small shop, and Mr. Hedges' is a large wholesale shop—it is in the ordinary course of business for small dealers to sell to large dealers.
WILLIAM WOODS. I am a clerk in the store-keeper's office, in Woolwich Dock-yard—I have looked at the copper produced—here is one piece with the broad-arrow on it, marked distinctly, and there are other pieces in which the broad-arrow appears to be defaced—these bolts generally appear to have been operate upon with a file—I conclude they have been field for the purpose of obliterating the mark, never having seen bolts so operated on before—the value of this copper would be about 3 l. 10 s. or 3 l. 12 s.—this pieces that is marked weight about 14lbs. and is worth 6 1/2 d. per pound—materials of this kind are never sold by Government, they are all sent to Portsmouth or Chatham to be re-manufactured.
Cross-examined. It must have been a very broad-arrow that has been filed out of this piece. A. The broad-arrow is stamped all over them, at about two inches or two inches and a half distance—it is not all over this piece, this may not have been a manufactured bolt—it is used for the purpose of sepating materials—I went to ask the men what it was used for to satisfy myself and any one that might ask—I did not know without asking.
MR. PETERSDORF. Q. Has it the broad-arrow on it? A. Yes.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY — Recommended to mercy by the Jury — Fined 5 l., Confined one Moths
[trialAccount t18470920-2224]
2224. ELIZABETH DABBS was indicted for stealing I watch, value 2 l.; and 1 watch-chin, value 2 d.; the goods of George Cock; and SARAH DABBS for feloniously receiving the same, knowing them to be stolen.
AMELIA COCK. I am the wife of George Cock, a pensioner—we live in East-street, Greenwich—on the 5th of Aug. I met my husband in a public-house—we
went along Trafalgar-road—we met Elizabeth Dabbs' father, and then the two prisoners—we walked along Trafalgar-road—my husband said he would have his dinner—I fetched it, and we all had a little, and had two pots of beer between five of us—we then went towards Woolwich—it rained. we turned back, and called in at the Fathers—my husband said I had better go home and get tea—I went home, and Elizabeth Dabbs followed me—I lighted the fire, and got tea—my husband came home—he and I, and Elizabeth Dabbs had tea and supper together—my husband said he would go to bed, and took his watch from the hook by the chimney-piece, wound it up, and hung it on the same hook again—Elizabeth Dabbs was standing tying her bonnet, and looking at the Chimney-glass—the instant he hung the watch up she snatched it, and ran away—I ran after her to the Red Lion—her mother, Sarah Dabbs, was standing at the entrance of the Red Lion—Elizabeth thrust both her hand in her mother's bosom—I saw the watch-chain hanging down—her mother closed her bosom—I said, "Give me the watch"—Sarah Dabbs said, "I wish I had it"—they went home—I went with them—Elizabeth's father was sitting in the ground-floor room, in the dark—they went in, and in two or three minutes all three came out into the yard, and the father said, "There will be a rare bother with this, I am glad I was not there"—he said to his daughter, "You have been in the Penitentiary—she said, "I know I have"—she said to me, "My mother will give it you in the morning."
Sarah Dabbs. You said you would not give me in charge. Witness. There was no such word said—the policeman said, "I see, the girl has bounced you out of your watch, so, old woman, you had better go home;" and two days afterwards that policeman was discharged for being a bad character.
GEORGE COCK. I had the watch—I would it up at a quarter before ten, and placed it on the hook again—it was perfectly safe—in a short time I saw Elisabeth Dabbs run out of the room with it—my wife ran after her, as I had got my boots off.
Elizabeth Dabbs. You know you were perfectly tipsy, you could not follow me. witness. I was perfectly sober.
Elizabeth Dabbs' Defence. I did not take it, nor did my mother; Mr. Cock asked me to make tea, as she was not able—I then went and told my mother to come; we wanted to have a pint of beer; my mother said, "I have got a penny in my bosom;" I was feeling for it; this women came, and said, "Did you see my husband's watch?" I said, "No;" my mother said, "I hope you do not accuse my daughter;" she said, "No;" the policeman and she came, searched the room, and it was not there; the policeman is not on duty now.
Sarah Dabbs' Defence. The policeman came, my wish; there was no watch; I said, "I hope you don't think my daughter has got it;" she said, "No; my husband is in the habit of putting it away, and he has done so;" I said, "Here is the policeman; "he came and searched, and said, "Shall I take them in charge?" she said, "No; my husband has mislaid it before, he may have done it again;" we went to two public-houses, and then to the Feathers; we had all had enough; Mrs. Cock swore the watch was on the table, and next time she it was on the hook.
AMELIA COCK re-examined. My husband never mislaid his watch—I never said so—we were all perfectly sober—Dabbs was at home in the dark when the prisoners went home—the door was open—they had no key.
ELIZABETH DABBS — GUILTY. Aged 17.— confined Three Months.
SARAH DABBS — GUILTY. Aged 44.— confine six Month. >

2226. CATHERINE HERION was indicted for stealing 1 pair of bellows, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 candlestick, 2 s. 6 d.; and 1 watch-guard, 4 d.; the goods of Phillip Kreckeler.
ESTHER KRECKELER. I am the wife of Phillip kreckeler—we keep a toy-shop, in London-street, Greenwich. On the 30th of Aug., between one and two o'clock, the prisoner came to me, and purchased a toilet-bottle—these was a pair of bellows, some china candlestick, and an India-rubber watch-guard on the counter—she was in the shop about half-an-hour—she only purchased the toilet-bottle—I served her—she gave me five sixpence—I did not miss anything after she was gone, but was sent for to Mrs. Smaill's, up the court by the side of my shop, and saw my property there—I had not sold it—nobody serves in the shop but me—these bellows, guard, and candle-sticks ( produced) are mine.
Prisoner. Q. Do you recollect what passed, when I purchased a chain of you? A. There was a chain sent home for your daughter to look at, and if she did not like it I was to take it back—you did not say you would pay for these thing when you got the chain—I did not say, "Take what you please, the money is sure"—your servant was with you—I had not told you to out at my shop at any time, and I would give you a glass of porter—I did say to you that the bellows were not new—you did not take them by my leave—your servant asked me to allow you to have a pint of porter, as you were not well—I had never seen you before—I found the things between six and seven o'clock—you were there between one and two.
CAROLINE KING. I live at No. 3, Burley-court, Greenwich. I have known the prisoner five months—on the 30th of Aug., between five and six o'clock, I went to Mrs. Smaill's, and saw the prisoner—she gave me a basket, and asked me to take care of it till she came home—I kept it till between six and seven, and gave it to Henry Chorley—this is it ( produced).
HENRY CHORLEY. I live at No. 3, Burley-court, Greenwich. King gave me a basket—I carried it down, and emptied the contents into Mrs. Smaill's apron—there was a china candlestick, a watch-guard, and a pair of bellows—these produced are them.
ELIZABETH SMAILL. I am the wife of Thomas Smaill, of Burely-court, Greenwich. I have known the prisoner about six months—on the 30th of Aug., in the afternoon, the prisoner came to my house—shortly afterwards I received a basket from Chorely—these things produced were in it—the prisoner was in the house, but was not present.
HENRY BURNS ( policeman.) On the afternoon of the 30th of Aug. I accompanied Mrs. Kreckeler to a house in Burley-court, saw the prisoner, and asked her where the bellows, candlestick, and watch-guard were—she said she had nothing in the world but what she had paid for—I asked where her basket was—she said, behind her—I found it, there was nothing in it—Chorley said in her presence, if I went down stairs he would bring me to where the property was—I went down, and received the articles from Mrs. Smaill—I went back to the prisoner, told her I had found the things, and took her to the station—she said she thought it was better for her to go—I think she had been drinking—she was not drunk then.
( The prisoner produced a written defence stating, that as she wanted the money she had in her pocket, the prosecutrix allowed her to take what she required upon her promising to pay for them.)
GUILTY. Aged 31.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Two Months

2230. JOHN NOBLE was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Gilpin, about one in the night of the 1st of Sept., at St. George the Martyr, Suothwark; with intent to steal.
MR. PRENDREGAST conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM GILPIN. I live in High-street, Borough, in the parish of St. George the Martyr, Southwark. About eleven o'clock, on the 1st of Sept., I went to bed—I fastened up my premises—the shop-door was bolted and locked—about one o'clock I was awoke by the door-bell ringing—I dressed, went down, and found two constable with the prisoner in custody—I looked round my premises with one of them, and found the first floor back-room window open—there is a court by the side of the house—the window goes out on the leads—the shop extends beyond the house—there is a skylight with leads, and whoever got into the house must have climbed up the water-spout, got on the leads, and then lifted up the back-room window—that could have been done—I found foot-marks on the leads, as if a person had had nails in his boots—it was a scraping on the leads—I had a very large stock—nothing was taken—a pair of steps had been placed against the shop-door at night—I found them two yards from the door, placed against the shutter-box—the window had been shut quite down, but not fastened—I saw the officer pick up a dark lantern in the shop—it was not on my premises before.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Is your dwelling-house at the shop? A. Yes; it is one building—there is only one door in the front, and no door at the back—there was no means of getting in at the back but by the window—when I came down the shop-door was shut, and the policemen were in the shop—there was quite a mob outside, and the policemen closed the door against them—I do not know the prisoner—I always close the window myself—it has a shutter—I had not shut the shutter for some time, but have done so since—I am quite certain I had fastened the window that night—there are gas-light near my shop—the street is very light all through.
DONALD THAIN ( policemen, M 246.) On this night I was passing along the Borough, and tried Mr. Gilpin's door to see if it was safe—it opened, and the prisoner rushed out of the shop into the street, and turned to the right toward Union-street—I ran after him—I saw him stopped just as he turned Union-street—I am quite sure the man that was stopped was the man I was in pursuit of, and I am quite sure the prisoner is the man who came out of the house—I produced a black bag, which I got from Apthorp.
Cross-examined. Q. How far is it from the prosecutor's house to when the prisoner was taken? A. About 130 yards—I lost sight of him for a moment as he turned into Union-street, and then found him in custody— —there was a cry of, "Stop thief!"—people were not running after him—there was a crowd after he was brought back in custody—I saw nobody run after him but myself and Bray—I took him—some more people might have been there—no one was close to him when I took him but Bray—I cannot say whether there were fifteen people within a few yards of him—I saw marks on the lead and window-cill—there was only one mark on the window-ledge—there were a good many on the wall outside—I only saw one at the place where the nails were visible—the wall is a about eleven feet high—the water-spout runs up it—I did not pursue anybody at the back.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Could anybody have been substituted for the prisoner between the time of your missing him for the moment, and turning the corner, and seeing him in the witness's custody? A. No, there was nobody else that could have been mistaken for him.
JESSE BRAY. I am clerk to an attorney, and live in Prince's-road, Kennington-cross. About one o'clock on the night in question. I was going along the Borough, and heard a rattle spring—I saw the prisoner running, pursued by the policeman—I did not stop him, but I saw him secured, and am sure he is the man—when he came up to me I stepped off the path, and attempted to stop him, but seeing his arm raised, I had an idea that he had a weapon—I stepped off the path, intending to attack him sideways—he struck at me, and I at him—he passed me, turned the corner, then turned with his face to the wall, and walked back again, as if going towards St. George's church—I did not lose sight of him, and am certain he is the man.
Cross-examined. Q. What were you doing out at this time of night? A. I was walking down the Borough, going home—I am in the employ of Mr. Devenport, of Ely-place—I was coming from Tooley-street, where I had been spending the evening with a few friends—I had drank nothing but porter, and not exceeding three pints of that—I have some slight knowledge of having seen the prisoner before, somewhere about the Elephant and Castle—he only turned the corner about three yards—I ran past him for about two yards—I saw nobody in Union-street but the prisoner, Bray, and myself—I went back with him to the prosecutor's—there was a crowd round the door afterwards, but not at the time I came up—a crowd followed us from where we took the prisoner into custody, about ten yards from the corner of Union-street.
WILLIAM APTHORPE. I am a private watchman at the Half Moon in the Borough. I was outside the gate of the Half Moon on the night in question, and saw the prisoner coming towards me, followed by the policeman, halloing, "Stop thief!"—I am sure he is the man—I saw him drop the bag produced from under his coat—I picked it up and gave it to the policeman when he came back with the prisoner in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you a watch-box there? A. No, I walk up and down the yard, and sometimes sit down—I had been on duty since nine o'clock—I was standing outside the gate at the time—[it is sleepy work—it is a very light neighbourhood.
WILLIAM PARNELL. ( policeman, M 5.) I saw the prisoner in company with two others, in the neighbourhood of Blackman-street, Borough, between half-past four or five o'clock on the afternoon in question—I am quite certain he is the person—he was only a short distance from the prosecutor's house—they were walking along together in the direction of St. George's church—I did not see him again till next morning.
Cross-examined. Q. Then you saw him in custody? A. Yes, in the cells—they were not walking in a direction towards the Surrey Theatre, but coming from it—I was going towards it—I met no other parties that afternoon that took my notice—I was not examined before the Magistrate.
DAVID HANSELL ( policeman, M 117.) I was on the spot, and picked up this lantern in Mr. Gilpin's shop, about two yards from the door.
MR. GILPIN re-examined. This is not my lantern—it was not there over night.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 26.— Confined One Year.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

2232. JANE HEWITT was indicted for stealing 42 yards of cotton, value 1 l. 3 s.; the goods of Richards Heman.
RICHARD HEMAN. I am a linen-draper, of Albion-terrace, Wandsworth On the 18th of Sept., about eleven o'clock at night, I missed a piece of cotton print—I had seen it safe ten minutes or a quarter of an hour before—I accompanied the policeman to the prisoner's lodgings, found her there, and the print also—this produced is it—she said she did not take it herself—I have a piece which was torn off the print, it corresponds with it.
Prisoner. Q. Have not I been at the shop a number of times?. A. I do not recollect, but it is very possible.
WILLIAM BARNARD. I am in Mr. Heman's service. On Saturday night about eleven o'clock, I was five or six yards from the door, and saw the prisoner come out to the door three or four times, and then go away very quickly.
EDWARD WIDDELL ( policeman.) On Saturday night, between ten and eleven o'clock, I saw the prisoner about 100 yards from Mr. Hemman's shop passing and repassing for an hour—I received information, and went with Mr. Heman to her lodging—I knew she lived there by seeing her them—I saw her, and told her she was suspected of stealing a piece of cotton print from Mr. Heman, and asked if she had any objection to my searching—she said, "No"—I searched, and found this cotton under the bed.
