[trialAccount t18470920-2233]
2233. MARY ANN WALLS was indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering a forged acquittance and receipt for the payment of 1 s. 2 d., with intent to defraud James Aldridge.
MR. P. TAYLOR conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES ALDRIDGE. The prisoner came into my service on the 2nd of June—I gave her money every week to pay the small tradesmen in the neighbourhood—I know Mr. Males, the beer-shop keeper—this now produced is a bill of his—I gave the prisoner the money to pay it—she returned me the bill receipted the same night or the next morning, and I put it on the file.
COURT. Q. What directions did you give her when you gave her the money? A. I laid the money for each bill upon it, and told her to go and pay each tradesman immediately—she went away, and afterwards brought me this receipt—( read —"June 27,847; Mr. Aldridge to Hugh Males, week's beer, 1 s. 2 d. Received, Hugh Males.")
HUGH MALES. I keep a beer-shop. This is a bill supplied to the prosecutor—it is in my hand-writing, with the exception of the receipt—that is not mine—I never authorized any person to put my name to that bill—it has not been paid.
COURT. Q. Had you ever received money from the prisoner? A. I have, about once, two months ago—my wife attends to my business in my absence, but never receipts any bills—she can write, but this is not her writing.
( The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that she was induced by the prosecutor to live with him as his wife; that, instead of paying the bills weekly, she was in the habit of letting them accumulate till they amounted to 10 s . or 12 s.; when she discharged them, and that she intended paying the bill in question on the day following her being taken into custody.)
JEMES ALDRIDGE re-examined. What the prisoner states is entirely false—I never had any familiar intercourse with her—she said would makes up this story for me and the rest of the witnesses.
GUILTY. Aged 41.— Confined Two Years.
(There were several other indictments against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18470920-2234]
2234. THOMAS SIMPSON was indicted for feloniously assaulting Alfred Lainson, putting him in fear, and taking from his person and against his will, I half-crown. 1 shilling, and 2 sixpences, his monies; and MARY GREEN as an accessary before the fact.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
ALRFED LAINSON. I am a carpenter, and live at 45, Portman-street, Vauxhall. On Saturday evening, the 21st of Aug., about twenty minutes to nine o'clock, I was in Battersea-fields, and overtook the prisoner Green—she spoke to me first, and said, "My dear, will you go with me"?—I told her, "No, I was going home"—I had never seen her before—I bade her good night—I did not stop, I merely turned round and walked on again—a boy then came up to me, crying, and saying he had lost a horse—I stopped to speak to him, and Green ran away—the prisoner Simpson then ran up to me, and said I had been with his wife, and if I did not give him something he would punch my head off, and he put himself in a fighting attitude—I told him I had not been with his wife, or any man's wife, and I would not give him anything—he put his fingers up to his mouth and whistled—I then saw several men running towards me from the tents in the field, and I ran away—they set a dog at me—in running away I stumbled over a mound and fell—Simpson then came up to me, and the other men also—there were about eight or twelve of them—I directly jumped up—he complained to the other men that I had been with his wife, and they all said I must give them some money—Simpson touched my waistcoat, and he and another man held out their hands, and said I must give them some money—I asked them to go to a public-house with me, and I would treat them with drink—I proposed that with a view of identifying some of them—they all said, "No, that will not do for us, you must give us the money now"—Simpson and another man then held out their hands, and I gave them half-acrown each—they then told me to go away from the field and never come there any more—I gave them the money out of fear—they threatened to punch my head if I did not—I went straight towards Battersea-bridge, met a policeman, and returned with him to the spot—we saw the prisoner Green in the Old-House-at-Home public-house in the field, but I did not speak to her—I had not taken any sort of liberty with the girl, or anybody else.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. This was somewhere about nine o'clock? A About twenty minutes to nine o'clock—it was about a quarter to nine o'clock when I met the policeman, and that was about five
minutes after this matter happened—it was dusk, getting dark—it was a moonlight night—there are no gas-lights in Battersea-fields—I saw Simpson on the Sunday following, the next day, in the field—I did not give him into custody, the policeman was too far behind, and he made his escape—I was in search of him with the policeman—I saw him in the field, putting stumps down in the booth—he walked very fast, and I missed him while I was looking after the policeman—I saw him again on the 2nd of Sept., when he was taken into custody—I was taken to the station by a policeman, to see him—he was pointed out to me—I was not at all drunk on the night in question.
COURT. Q. How far was Simpson from you when you saw him in the field on the Sunday? A. Within a yard or two—there is a fair held there on Sundays—I saw him scarcely for a minute—he went into the booth, and out at the other end—he saw me, and appeared to know me—he coloured is the face, and while I was making a sign to the policeman he disappeared—I am quite sure he saw me—the policeman was about 100 yards behind me, coming across the field—I communicated this to the policeman as soon as I got up to him.
Green. How can you say he was putting stumps in the booth when I was down there on the very same Sunday evening, and he had cock-shy sticks in his hand? Witness. They were stumps—I did not see you in the field that day—I did not give you any money.
THOMAS BENT ( policeman V 95.) I heard of this robbery on the Tuesday, and looked for the prisoner Simpson—I saw him on Thursday, the 2nd of Sept., in the Wandsworth-road—I asked whether he did not go by the name of Swanney —he said he did—I said he was charged with robbing a man in Battersea-fields on the Saturday night week before—he said. "I was not them at all that night"—I said I must take him to the station to see whether he was or not—I had known him before by the name of Thomas Simpson, and by the nickname of Swanney.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he say, "I was not there that night; I was not near the place?" A. Yes.
JHON WALLIS ( policeman, V 293.) I was on duty in Battersa-foelds on the evening of the 21st of Aug., and about five minutes before nine o'clock met the prosecutor—he told me what had happened to him, in consequence of which I went back with him—we saw the female prisoner, but I did not speak to her or take her into custody then—I saw her again on the 3rd of Sept., and took her into custody—I told her she was charged with being concerned in a robbery in Battersea-fields with Swanney Simpson—she said she knew nothing about Swanney Simpson—next day, after coming out of the Magistrate's office, she said there was only her and Simpson and the prosecutor there, only them three—no one was putting questions to her at that time—she was in custody at the time—we were returning from the Magistrate's office—she said she wanted to give her evidence—I told her she had been asked twice whether she had anything to ask the witnesses, and she had said as—she said she wished to tell the truth, that there was only them three there, that was Simpson, her, and the prosecutor.
Cross-examined. Q. Only three, where? A. In Battersea-fields, at the time of the robbery—I believe "there" was the word she used—I am sure that was the word—"there at the time"—I was only examined once before the Magistrate, that was on the same day that Green was examined—what she said was on coming away from the examination—she had been remanded from the day before, for her and Simpson to be brought up together.
COURT. Q. Were you before the Magistrate more than once? A. Yes—I was there after Green stated this to me—I made a mistake when I said I was only examined once before the Magistrate—I was examined twice—it was the second time that she said what I have stated.
Green. I never said whether there were three or four persons. Witness. She did, and the prosecutor heard her as well—she said she wished t tell the truth, that there was only Simpson and her and the prosecutor there at the time—I swear she said that, and she heard me state it to the Magistrate.
JOHN POTTER ( policeman, V 118.) On the night of the 21st of Aug. I was on duty in Battersea-fields, and saw Simpson there about a quarter-past eight o'clock—I knew him, and had seen him there the whole of the season—he has got cock-shies there—I have seen Green about there with a number of other persons, stopping out there—there is a kind of fair there the whole of the summer, with horse-riding, swings, target-shooting, with nuts, and all that kind of thing.
WILLIAM JOHNSON. I keep a coffee-stall in Battersea-fields. I know the prisoners by sight, but nothing more—I have not been there long—I begun to keep the stall about two months ago, and have seen them in my stall every day for the last two months, sometimes apart and sometimes together.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you seen Green with other men? A. No, not unless they were in company together—I have not seen Simpson with other people—I never noticed him, except in her company—if she came for any refreshment when he was down in field, she had it and went away—sometimes they both came together.
COURT. Q. You have seen a good many other people in these fields during the two months? A. Yes, hundreds of the same rank in life—it was nothing particular to see him there on the 21st of Aug., more than any one else—I was at home, no board my barge in the dock in Battersea-fields on Saturday night, at nine o'clock—I did not know anything about this till Monday.
MR. RYLAND. Q. What time did you give up business in your stall? A. About nine o'clock—the stall is in the barge—I live there.
ALFRED LAINSON re-examined. I was in the girl's company about a minute and a half or two minutes—I did not look at her before she spoke to me, or look round at her as I passed—I was turning my head round, and was just about walking on, when the boy came up to me—he was crying very bitterly, which attracted my attention—when he ran away, she ran away too, and the men ran towards me—I had not much opportunity of looking at her face, but I knew her again directly I saw her—I had never seen her before—I saw her again the same evening—it was all done in three, four, or five minutes—the male prisoner whistled almost directly he came up—he stood facing me, in a fighting attitude—the moon was shining, and I could distinguish his countenance well—I had seen him as I was crossing the field, in the early part of the evening, in company with another man, and recollected, having seen him before that day—I am quite satisfied he is the same person.
THOMAS BENT re-examined. I was before the Magistrate on the 8th, when the prisoners made a statement—they were asked severally whether they had anything to say—Green made a statement, which was taken down, and read over to her—she made her mark, and the Magistrate signed it—I know his writing—Simpson also made a short statement, which was witnessed by the Magistrate.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hear Wallis examined? A. Yes, on the 4th—I apprehended Green on the 3rd—to the best of my belief, he went through the whole of his examination again on the 8th—I might not have been there the whole of the day—I gave no evidence that day—I have no doubt I was there the whole of the time—I was there at the time the deposition were read—I do not think I left the Court at all—I believe I went out once with the male prisoner—the examination was not going on then—I do not think I was called out of Court—I heard Wallis make his statement—he gave the whole of his evidence on the 8th—I do not remember what evidence he gave—the prisoners were told that they might say what they liked, but what they said would be taken down and used against or for them at the trial—Mr. Beadon was the Magistrate—I have seen him write many times for two or three months—I stood close to him, and saw him write this—I know the character of his writing—I have seen him sign my recognisance papers—I should say this is not his writing ( looking at a paper)—I will not swear to it—I have not seen him write like that—I have a doubt about this being his writing—he sometimes writes similar to this, but not like this—he writes "William Frederic Beadon," but not with a lot of dashes under it—( The prisoners' statements were here read —"The prisoner Green says, 'On last Saturday fortnight, between seven and eight o'clock, the gentleman met me and asked me how I did, and asked me to take his arm—he went with me to the Red House, and treated me—we went across the fields—he said he had taken a fancy to me, and he would be a good friend to me, but he was rather short of cash that night, that he had only 3 s. 6 d. in his waistcoat pocket—he gave me 3 s., and said he would keep the 6 d., as we should want a glass of brandy-and-water—he took me across the bank, and began taking indecent liberties with me—I would not let him—he wanted me to give him the money back—I said it was a gift, but I would go to a house with him—he said he would knock me down if I did not let him do as he liked—I said, 'If you do, I shall get somebody to do it for you'—he struck me on the shoulder—I called out, and Simpson ran from the booth—I told him, and he told the gentleman he had better go on about his business, and he went on—I went on, and saw the prosecutor fall down over the bank—I went to the Old House-at-Home, and treated Simpson for his protection—I asked him to see me across the fields—he saw me as far as the Duchess public-house, and left me—I saw the gentleman on the Tuesday after, and because I would not let him go with me, he said he would punish me and the man too—The prisoner Simpson says, 'What she has said is true, he never gave me any money.' ")
(Thomas Holden, plumber, of New Compton-street, Soho, gave Simpson a good character.)
SIMPSON — GUILTY. Aged 21.
GREEN — GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Justice Williams.
[trialAccount t18470920-2235]
2235. RICHARD HAYNES was indicted for feloniously assaulting Jane M'Casland, and wounding her, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
MR. MELLER conducted the Prosecution.
JANE M'CASLAND. I live with my mother, Susan M'Casland, at 29, Lant-street, Southwark. On the 26th of July, about half-past eight o'clock in the evening, I was in Mint-street, going to get a jug of hot water, and saw the prisoner leading a woman along—he was not drunk, but the woman was—I
passed, and turned round to look at them—I did not speak to them—the prisoner dropped the person he was carrying, and ran up and kicked me in the bottom of my belly—I cannot recollect whether he said anything, for I lost my senses, and fell into a gentleman's arms—I did not recollect myself till I found myself in my mother's bed—I have been confined to my bed ever since.
Prisoner. Q. What did you say when I had hold of my mother? A. I did not speak to you—I did not say you had kicked your mother—I did not know you had done so—I am quite sure I did not speak to you or interfere.
CHARLES SOMERVILLE. I am a porter, in the Borough-market, and live in Mint-street. On Sunday evening, the 25th of July, about half-past eight o'clock, I was in Mint-street, right opposite out door, and saw the prisoner assault the prosecutrix—she fell into my arms—the prisoner was leading his mother in his arms along the road, about five yards from me, and the children sat up a hooting, seeing her drunk—the prosecutrix was coming along with an empty jug in her hand—the prisoner was coming one way, and she the other—the prisoner stood still, and she looked him in the face, but never said a word—in about a minute, he said, "If you don't get out of the way, you young b----, I will kick your guts out of you," and he no sooner said that than he dropped his mother on the pavement; ran towards the girl directly, and kicked her at the same moment—I went to assist her—she was about five yards from me—I caught her in my arms, if I had not, she would have dropped on the kerb-stone, and cut her head open—she was in a fainting state—I got a man to assist me, and took her home.
COURT. Q. Did you say there were other children there? A. Yes, several—they were hooting at him before the girl came up—they were laughing and hallooing at seeing a drunken woman—she was so drunk that it was a sight to see her—she could not stand—the children were not all together—this girl was quite by herself, with the jug in her hand—she was on this side, against a shop, more in the gutter—the other children were more across the road, making a noise—she did not say a word, or do anything, that I observed—I should have seen or heard her if she had—she was about five yards from me, and four yards from the prisoner—when he had kicked her, he ran away directly, and left his mother to herself.
Prisoner. My mother was solid and sober; it was me that was tipsy. Witness. His mother was quite drunk—he had had a little drop, but was perfectly conscious of what he did—he ran away directly—it happened right opposite my door—I did not see him come from the public-house, or where he came from—it was not done opposite the public-house, but opposite the chandler's shop, on the left-hand side of the public-house, three or four doors from it.
Prisoner. If you stood there, you must have heard the words the girl made use of; but you were not there, you were at your door, and I was at the public-house, and there are five or six doors between. Witness. No, only three—it was right facing my door—I am quite sure she did not speak a word to you.
CHRISTIANA EDENEY. I am wife of Edward Edeney, of Mint-street. On Sunday evening, the 25th of July, I saw the prisoner in the road with a woman, and the prosecutrix going for some water—the mug was standing before her—the prisoner laid his mother down in the road, the girl was standing before her, he made use of a very bad expression, kicked her at the bottom of the belly, and told her to be off—she fell back, and would have
fallen on the kerb-stone had not Somerville caught her—I saw her takes home—I followed her, and saw a deal of blood on her clothes—I was there when the doctor examined her.
COURT. Q. Did you hear the children hooting at the prisoner? A. No, they were kicking up a great noise before this happened—I did not hear the girl say anything, she was only looking on—I am quite sure she did not say a word, nor was she making any noise—she was not with the children who were making a noise—she was going past, and was going to turn to look at the woman on the ground.
Prisoner. Q. Where were you? A. Against my own door—I lodge there—that is the statement I made to Mr. Cottingham—I said I was opposite Mr. Shea's house, not that lived there—I did not wish to say where I lived, because I knew I should be annoyed by the people that lived in the house—I said it was immaterial where I lived, I lived just by there.
GEORGE ELISHA NEWTH. I am a surgeon, and live in Great Suffolk-street. On the 20th of July I was called to attend the prosecutrix—I found her lying on the bed, very faint—I examined her person, and found that the bottom of her belly, in the * * *, it was a wound about an inch and a half long, and was bleeding very much—there was a wound and a bruise—there was a good deal of blood on her person—in my opinion, it was a dangerous wound—she is suffering from it now very much—the bladder was very much affected, and is now—as soon as she came to, she complained of very great pain at the bottom of the belly, great pain on pressure—I administered proper remedies—she is has complained more or less since of that pain—she at first suffered from not passing her water, but now she has great difficulty in holding it, and has great pain when she passes it—she feels much easier when she is in a recumbent posture—she has been confined to her bed up to this time—in my opinion, such a contusion and wound might have been caused by the kick of a powerful man—if it had been a little higher, it might have ruptured the bladder—she is certainly recovering now, but I expect she will feel the effect of it almost ever after.
COURT. Q. When you say there was a wound, do you mean to say that the skin was broken? A. Yes—I am quite sure it amounted to a wound—it bled—it could not have bled without the skin breaking—it must have been a very violent kick.
Prisoner. Q. You told Mr. Cottingham it was not much of a wound, and not near the bottom of the belly, and that very trifling blood ran from it? A. I never said anything of the kind—the Magistrate said if there was any danger to her life I was to get further opinion, that the prisoner might have the benefit of all the medical advice that could be obtained, because, if the girl died, he perhaps might be committed for murder; but as the girl was getting better the mother did not wish anybody else called in.
WILLIAM GRIST ( policeman, M 223.) I took the prisoner, and produce one of his boots—it has large nails in it—he said he was going into the country to work, and knew nothing about it.
SUSAN M'CASLAND. I am the prosecutrix's mother. On the 25th of July I sent her for a mug of pump water—she was quite well when she went—she was brought home senseless—she has been confined to her bed ever since, and hardly able to sit up more than a few minutes in the course of the day.
Prisoner. Q. Was not she at Mr. Sutton's funeral? A. No, she was in bed.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in a public-house drinking with some parties;
my mother came, and said "Come home, and get your supper, because you must go to work early in the morning, and are three-parts drunk now;" I said if I came home I should lose a day's work, and asked her to go home and cook my victuals for me; she did so, and came back for me; I called for a glass of rum; as we came out somebody struck her; she told me of it, and I went out to find who it was; I wanted to get her home, and she went off in a fit; I could not get her on; this wench comes up, and said, "That man has kicked his mother;" I said, "My lady, the best thing you can do is to go away, and don't keep cheeking me;" she kept on saying, "That is the man that kicked his mother;" I took off my cap, and hit her with it; she did not offer to move, and I up with my foot, and kicked her jug.
MARY ANN BROWN. Eight weeks ago last Sunday I saw the prisoner intoxicated several times in the course of the day—at half-past seven in the evening I went into a public-house, where the prisoner and his mother were, for a pint of beer—I saw him at the bar persuading his mother to go home—they were both intoxicated a good deal.
GUILTY of a Common Assault. Aged 28.— Confired Six Months.
Before Mr. Baron Platt.
[trialAccount t18470920-2235b]
2235. JOHN SAUNDERS was indicted for stealing 1 sheep, value 1 l. 18 s.; the property of John Charles Watson.
WILLIAM LYNES ( policeman R 238.) On Wednesday, the 25th of Aug., I was in a field near Nunhead Cemetery, Deptford—I was dressed in a dark coat, not in my uniform—May, another officer, was with me in plain clothes—I got into a field, and found the prisoner skinning a sheep at the further corner, about a yard from the hedge—he was standing up with a knife in his hand, and the sheep between his legs—I said, "It is a fine night"—he said, "Yes"—I said, "Are you skinning the sheep?"—he said, "Yes—I said, "You are not much hand at skinning a sheep"—he said, "No"—I said, "Lend me the knife"—he asked me if I was a good hand at skinning a sheep—I said, "Yes, I have skinned and dressed a good many for Mr. Covel, of Lewisham; I was two years at it"—he said, "I think I shall be able to manage it"—I said, "Put the knife down, and take hold of the skin, and pull it towards the head, it will come off"—he did not put down the knife, but he took hold of the skin, pulled it up towards the head, and it came off as far as the neck—he then cut the head off, and put the knife down—I then stepped on the knife, took hold of him by the collar, and told him I was a policeman in plain clothes—May took hold of him at the same time—he tried to make his escape from us—with a deal of trouble we tied his hands together, and took him to the station—when I took him, he said he had found the sheep dying and stuck it; that he had a large family, and was out of employment—he had a steel with him—the sheep was quite warm—I suppose it was stuck about seven minutes before I came up—there was a quantity of blood about a yard form the sheep—I put my hand on the blood, it was quite warm—the prisoner had two rush baskets with him, and two or three towels in them, a hoe laid close by him—there was a bruise on the sheep's head from a blow—there were more sheep in the field—there was no public footpath in the field—I took the sheep to Mr. Allaway, a butcher, at Deptford, and he dressed it—I took the skin and head with me to the station-house—the skin was produced before the Magistrate.
Prisoner. Q. You asked me what I was going to do with it. A. I did
not; you said it was dying when you stuck it—there have been three sheep slaughtered besides this, and one taken away.
MAY ( policeman, R 296.) I have heard Lynes' evidence—it is correct—I was near enough to hear all that passed.
CHARLES WATSON. I am a butcher, in Kendall-street, Old Kent-road—I turned out fifteen sheep into a field of Mr. Cheshire's, about the 2nd of Aug., I afterwards took eight to be slaughtered, seven remained in the field on the night in question—I heard of the prisoner's being found in the field, about one or two that same morning—the two policemen came and informed me—next day I went to the field, and one of the sheep was missing—there were only six there—I went to Mr. Allaway's that same day, and there saw the carcase—I identified the skin at the station-house that same day—this now produced is it—it has the same mark that I put on all my sheep, the wool is just the same, and the brand mark "P"—I believe it to be the skin of one of my sheep—it was a Kent wether sheep, worth 38 s.—I went into the field, and saw some blood by the side of the ditch in the corner by the hedge, as if the sheep had been stuck there.
Prisoner's Defence. The hoe I took with the intention of getting work is turnip-fields; the basket I intended to sell, and had been to two or three pawnbrokers with it, but they would not buy it; I was going home at night, and got lost; I went up this place to lie down, it appeared to me like a wood, there being trees there; the sheep laid in the ditch; I felt it, it was warm; I made no more to do, but cut its head off, which I had done to many a one before, though not for the same purpose; I lived with a gentleman who bought upwards if 20,000 a year, and he always told me whenever I found a sheep that had been dead some hours to cut its head off; if I had not been in a state of starvation I should not have done it; I had been two or three days without anything for my children; I know I was wrong in so doing; I had not had any meat for upwards of a month, and for eight weeks not above a pound; still I did not kill the sheep, neither did I steal it, and my intention was to take part of it at a time; it was impossible for me, in the weak state I was, to catch a sheep, neither is there one butcher's man in twenty that can do it.
GUILTY. Aged 41.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Baron Platt.
[trialAccount t18470920-2236]
2236. JOHN AUGUSTUS OLDS was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Mary Olds; He was also charged upon the Coroner's Inquisition with the like manslaughter.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
FREDERICK RICHARD GOWAR. I am a surgeon, in the Old Kent-road. I knew the deceased Mrs. Olds very well—I was called to see her on the Thursday, the day she died—I had attended her before, for violet cough, occasional spitting of blood, and irritability of the lungs—it was a pulmonary complaint—my first observation of her on this occasion was on the Wednesday morning, 25th of Aug., when she came to my house, and complained of violent pain in her chest and about the stomach, and vomiting of blood at times—while she was in my house she spat up a good deal of blood—I never knew her spit blood before—she had frequently complained of cough and cold—she complained of being hurt about the throat, and being held and pressed about the chest—I saw the throat—the skin appeared a little reddened, as if compressed, but there was nothing particularly marked about it—it seemed redder
than it ought to be—there were no bruises—it was only red in one part—the left temple was bruised—that seemed to have been the effect of some violence—it was rather a lasting bruise, but not of itself likely to occasion death—I prescribed for her, and directed her to take everything very cold—I was sent for to her the next day, between one and two o'clock, and she was dead—I was prevented from seeing her in the morning—there was venous blood about the mouth—it seemed to come from the lungs—it was of a dark colour—by the direction of the Coroner I made a post-mortem examination—the appearance of the lungs was very unhealthy—the upper part of each lung was very congested, very loaded with extravasated blood, the bronchial vessels also very loaded with blood and the aesophagus—the blood in the lungs was partly fluid and partly coagulated—the liver was very unhealthy, of a very dark colour instead of its natural colour—there was a flaxidity about it, which showed a great deal of unnatural character—the gall-bladder was almost empty—there was scarcely any bile in it—it contained a little air apparently—the stomach appeared partially inflamed about the upper part, and there was a great deal of blood mixed with fluid of the stomach—it must have come into the stomach from the trachea—the vessels were very loaded with blood, not ruptured, but the arteries of that part are so minute, in all probability the blood might have come in partially from the veins of the stomach—that would not account for the whole—a large quantity must have come from the lungs, and got down the throat—the spleen and other organs were not quite healthy—there did not appear any inflammation about the bowels in general—the principal seat of disorder appeared the lungs and liver, and the irregular state of the bladder—in my opinion the flow of blood from the lungs arose from violence by blows or falls—the lungs being in a weak state they were susceptible—I can undertake to say that violence must have produced those effects—they could not have been produced to that extent without violence.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long had you attended this poor woman? A. Several times in the course of the summer—she had taken medicine—she had stated to me before this that she had spit small quantities of blood, not to any extent—she told me that she occasionally expectorated blood—I mean by that, thrown it from her lungs—that might probably be occasioned by ulceration—there must have been some lesion of vessels to give rise to it—there may be a lesion without a rupture, or the evacuation of blood, but it generally happens—I am able to say that the blood evacuated was venous blood, and not arterial—I did not ascertain what vessel was broken—there were a variety of vessels, towards the upper part of the lungs, not any of the main vessels—there is no difficulty in ascertaining whether blood is venous or arterial—the colour of venous blood is dark, and arterial blood is red or florid—at the upper part of the lungs, the veins and arteries mix and intersect—I can hardly say that there were no arterial vessels ruptured, probably there might have been, but the greater amount was venous blood—I cannot say whether that was from the diseased part of the lung, as the lungs altogether were very weak, and participated in point of texture to a similarity of construction—there had been ulceration—it occasionally happens with the lungs in such a state, that the cough may rupture the vessels and produce great extravasation of blood—it is almost universally the mode in which such patients die—I know that the deceased was subject to a frequent cough—I do not consider, from the appearances of the lungs, that a violent fit of coughing would have produced the great effusion that I witnessed; the lungs were apparently not in that very tender state as to produce it—they were in a weakened state—external violence must be a considerably greater strain
upon the lungs than a cough—it must in my opinion have been done by a violent concussion which jarred the vessels, and gave rise to the ruptures—such a concussion might be occasioned by a violent cough under a very much more diseased state of lings, but I do not consider these lungs were in that state—none of the main vessels were ruptured, only the smaller once—they would be very tender, and were in a delicate state.
Q. Suppose she had died from any other cause than the rupture, and you had seen these lungs without the extravasation of blood upon them, should you have said they were at all safe even from day to day? A. Yes—I should not consider they were in such a weak state as to give way—I should not consider that a violent cough would have been likely to affect them at all
Q. Evan though you had heard that she had been in the habit of spiting blood? A. The answer to that question would depend entirely upon the examination of any particular part which might be diseased—I believe har age was about forty odd, I cannot say exactly, from forty to fifty—she might have been fifty-two—the blood in the stomach had come from the lungs, got into the trachea, and so passed into the stomach.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Do I understand you rightly, that the state of the lungs was not in your opinion such as to lead you to conclude that a mere fit of coughing would have ruptured the vessels, and have produced the quantity of blood you saw? A. Yes—if on the Saturday night previous to my seeing her, she had been thrown down on the ground, and held there, I should consider that would account for the rupture of the vessels I saw.
COURT. Q. Were there many ulcers on the lungs? A. Not a great many, there were four or five patches of ulceration—sometimes the efflux of blend arises from ulceration eating into the vessels, opening the surface of the vessels themselves; and sometimes a vessel will burst or rupture from concussion, by reason of its weakness—it was quite impossible for me to investigate the number of opening through which the blood might have flowed, there was such a large accumulation in the parts that it was nothing but a mass of blood, intermixed so firmly in the upper part of the lungs with the trachea, and so on, that it was a complete choak as it were—I divided the lungs in my examination, but could discriminate the openings, the vessels are so very minute there—suffocation was the immediate cause of death by the vomiting of blood into the mouth, choaking the passage—she was coughing at the time she came to me in the morning from the quantity of blood that was rising in her throat—I had never seen her so violently troubled by a cough before—I had seen her about a fortnight before—I did not observe any more chance of her dying then, than I had observed at other periods when I had seen her—if I had heard next morning that she had been taken with a violent cough which had ended in suffocation by blood, I should have been very much surprised—she was not in that condition—she was generally emaciated, with weak powers, and had been in a bad state of health for several years—there was no accounting for the state of the liver, lungs, spleen, and gall-bladder—there was peculiar constitutional irregularity—I had know her for many years, and she was a very regular, quiet, domestic person—the liver may be disordered by a habit of drinking—there were appearances that would arise from irregular habits, but I never saw her intoxicated, or supposed she was addicted to drinking—I always considered her a woman of feeble habits, weak about the lungs, with an unnatural state of liver, and no on—the ordinary immediate cause of death from pulmonary complaints is from inflammation of the substance of the lungs, producing matter, so that
they expectorate pus and in proportion to the number of ulcers on the surface of the lungs, so in the same ratio will a person become sooner or later destroyed—it is a diminution of the powers of life—the process of ulceration going on in the lungs must necessarily furnish blood to support those inflammatory parts, and thus nature is robbed of a very great support—it decays the functions of the lungs—I examined the deceased's body externally—I did not perceive any marks of violence except that on the left temple and the throat.
LOUISA WILCOXON. I am the wife of Henry Wilcoxon, a brush-maker, living at 1, Wood's-court, John-street, Newington—I knew the deceased Mrs. Olds and her husband, they lived in the next house to me. On the Saturday night the 21st Aug., I was in my own back room, and was disturbed by a fall, and a cry of murder—I heard the fall first—the deceased and her husband slept in the up-stairs front room—the fall appeared to be in their back room—I heard Mrs. Olds cry "Murder"—I then ran to my door and opened it—the cries were still going on—they then appeared to come from the front room—I went up stairs into my front room, and opened the window, and then heard Mr. Olds say something to his wife, but what it was I could not understand—I heard her crying—I did not hear her say anything—it appeared to me as though he was still beating her—I did not see him—I judged by her cries that he was beating her—I did not hear the sound of blows just then—I saw their son William pass my door as I opened it—he came from his father's house—I saw him come back with a policeman—after the policeman was gone, I heard Mr. Olds beating his wife again—I heard the blows—the policeman stayed, I should think, about five minutes—I heard Mrs. Olds say, "You brute," you brute," several times, that was after I heard the sound of blows—there was no one there but the boy William, Mr. Olds, and his wife—I only heard the one fall, the other was hitting—I saw the deceased about four o'clock on the afternoon of the next day (the Sunday) in the back yard—she was very ill then—I saw her again on the Monday in the back yard, and saw marks on her throat—I did not see them on Sunday—I was nearer to her on Monday than I was on the Sunday—she had a bruise on the left temple—I had seen her about the middle of the day on the saturday—she had not any bruise on the left temple then—I was her on the Wednesday and Thursday—she was very ill each day—each day she got worse—I saw her at the sink vomiting blood—she called me to see it—I had not seen her spit blood before the Saturday—she had not been well for some time before—after the Saturday night I observed a very great change in her—it continued up to Thursday, when she died—I saw her on the morning she died, at nine o'clock, not after that—she died at half-past two—I ran in when she was dying.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you intimate with her? A. Yes—I generally saw her every day—she used to go out washing I believe—she did not go out to wash after this, and had not been for some weeks before, in consequence of the state of her heath—she said she had no strength—she appeared not well—she had a very bad cough—it was a very teasing cough, lasting for some minutes at a time—it appeared to strain her and try her very much—I did not see that she spat blood when she coughed—I have seen her spit after the coughing, but have not noticed whether there was—blood or not—it did not seem to be matter—she was a very sober woman—I never saw her the worse for liquor—I heard the prisoner say something to her, but what it was I could not understand—he was tipsy—when he was tipsy I could not understand him—he was tipsy on Saturday night.
MR. GOWAR re-examined. Hemorrhage on the lungs produced by violence does not diminish in general—I never knew it do so—if the violence was great I should think it likely that the hemorrhage would continue; but there are so many shades, it depends entirely upon the degree of violence, and the injury occasioned by that violence—in this instance it seemed to increase, under a certain state it would diminish—the natural tendency would be to heal—I do not consider the blow on the temple likely to have produced this hemorrhage—I attribute the redness of the neck to squeezing—that alone was not sufficient to occasion the hemorrhage, nor do I consider that the two combined were sufficient—there was no evident mark of any blow, or pressure, or violent impact on the head—there was extravasation about the neck and shoulder, resulting from the following of the blood from the lungs, but that was from the position of the body after death—if she was seized by the throat, and forced violently upon the ground, I think that might have produced the mischief, by a violent jarring of the system—if that might were so, it would be natural to expect to find some mark on the back, but I saw none, nor any bruise—a person might be pushed down, and receive a violent inward shock, without any external appearance of it whatever—I cannot say that the same concussion would be produced, if in a struggle she fell down, as if she had been thrown down and pressed upon—I found nothing to indicate that she had been cut down and passed upon.
ELIZA OLDS. I am the wife of Joseph Olds, a bricklayer, and live at No. 3, John-street, next door to the prisoner; I married his son. On the saturday night before the deceased's death I heard a piece of work there—the youngest boy, William, came to me; and, in consequence of what he said I went in—I found the prisoner and the deceased there alone—the deceased opened the door the moment I got there, and the prisoner shut it again—the deceased went towards the door again—the prisoner would not let her open it—he took her round the waist, and she fell down—she desired me to open the window—I jumped on the table to open it, and Mr. Palmer jumped in at the window—he lives in the same street—my mother was then on the floor by the front door, and my father by her—I do not know whether he had hold of her or not—in my fright I did not see—I do not know how she got on the floor—she was not on the floor when I jumped on the table to open the window, but when I opened it and Mr. Palmer jumped in, she was, and the prisoner had hold of her, I did not see in what way—Mr. Palmer went away before I did—I remained with the deceased—my husband came in, and took the prisoner away—he was gone about an hour and a half—I had not seen the deceased on the Friday—I had seen her on the Thursday—I was with her on the Sunday—she did not appear to be very well then—she complained of being very stiff and sore—she was up, and down stairs on Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday, and on the Thursday also—she seemed as if she got very weak and had—she had been very ill for a long time, and getting worse—she had been getting weaker and weaker every day up to this times—she got worse and worse from the Sunday till the Thursday—she died about half-past two o'clock on the Thursday—she was choked, through vomiting blood—I went for the doctor, but she was dead before he came—the prisoner was quite tipsy on the Saturday night.
Cross-examined. Q. While you were in the room, you did not see your father strike your mother at all? A. No, I did not see any blows—I did not see him use any violence at all towards her while I was there—she did not call out as if any violence was used towards her, but I did not come up till it was over—when the deceased was on the ground I do not know whether the
prisoner was trying to help her up or not—I had observed the deceased spitting during the last eight or nine months, and every time she spat she spat blood—that had been increasing—she said she spat more every day, I did not see it—I had seen her spit blood before this Sunday, but not that quantity that she did at last—I saw her spit blood on the Monday night—I do not know whether she did so on the Saturday—I did not see her till the Saturday night—I saw her spit a little on the Sunday—she spat more blood on the Monday than she had done before—not a great deal more—I had never seen her spit so much before—the prisoner was almost always very kind to her, and to me and the rest of the family, for whatever I saw.
MR. PAYNE. Q. You did not live with him? A. No Monday night, and—I did not see the deceased at the sink on the Monday—I saw her on the saw her spit blood in the fire-place—I had seen her spit blood before the Saturday—she mostly spat it as she sat by the fire-place—there was mostly phlegm with it—I never saw anything like what occurred on the day she died, or like what occurred on the Monday night.
COURT. Q. Was it worse on Monday than it was on Sunday. A. Yes; it was getting worse every day, and on the Thursday she was suffocated—on the Sunday I observed a bruise on her forehead, over the eye—I saw no mark on her neck—she never showed me any mark—I never saw it—I did not see it after her death.
MR. PAYNE. Q. How was she dressed about the throat? A. She had a gown that came high up in the neck, and she always had a handkerchief on under that, so that you could not see her neck—I did not see the body after death—my mother was with her when she died—I was not—her name was Mary.
WILLIAM HENRY PALMER. I am a bricklayer, living in John-street—I knew the deceased. On the Saturday night before she died, about half-past nine o'clock, I was standing at my own door, and heard a noise from the court where the prisoner lived—I went to the house, and found the prisoner and his wife quarrelling—the wife said to me, "For God's sake, take him away; for I am afraid he will kill me," or something to that effect—I went in on two occasions—the first time I went in by the door, nobody was there then but the prisoner and his wife—I remained about five minutes that time—I said to the prisoner, "What is all this notice about?"—he said, "Oh, it is all right, old fellow!"—I said, "Well, put on your jacket, and come out with me; what is the use of quarrelling?"—he said, "No, I will go up stairs to bed"—I then came out—I went again from a quarter to half-an-hour afterwards, because there was a second disturbance—the door was shut then, and I got in by the window—I then saw the prisoner, the deceased, and the last witness in the room—I was there scarcely a minute that time—the son came in, and directly he came in I came out—the deceased seemed in a dreadfully agitated state, the first time I went in; and so did the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Did she complain of having received any injury or any violence whatever? A. She did not, she mad no complaint of any kind, no further than saying, "Take him away"—he appeared to have been drinking too much, and was in a very excited state.
JOHN KILLEEN ( policeman, P 309.) On the Saturday night in question the prisoner's son came to fetch me, and I went to the house about half-past nine o'clock—I saw the deceased there, and no one else—I saw a bruise on her face, I believe over the right eye; she drew my attention to it—that
was all she pointed out to me, and all I observed—she made a complaint to me, but did not charge anybody.
ANN NORTON. I am the wife of Stephen Norton. I knew the deceased—I went there on the Thursday, the day she died—I was present when she died, and assisted in laying her out—I had seen her that same morning, but not lately before that—I found her sitting in the chair—she said she was very ill—I went away at that time—I was sent for a half-past eight o'clock and found her in her daughter's arms vomiting blood—she died in my arms directly afterwards.
WILLIAM OLDS. I am fourteen years old, and lived with the prisoner and the deceased, my father and mother. On the Saturday night before she died I was at home—my father and mother and I were in the house above nine o'clock, nobody else—my mother had had some money left her—I believe it was kept in a drawer of a cheat of drawers that was there—when my father came home he said, "I want some money"—he was not sober my mother said, "No," she would not give it him; and he took hold of the drawers by the bottom and turned them over—I believe the money was in those drawers—I never saw any there—my father went backwards to get the hammer to break the drawers open—my mother took the hammer out of his hand, and threw it over the pigstye—he then began quarrelling and hitting her—they were both quarrelling—I did not see my mother hit him, nor my father hit her—I went out to fetch a policeman—my mother was then in the front room—she was standing up—I did not see her down upon the ground at all—I went for the policeman because my mother sent me—I did not see my father do anything to her—she was sitting down in a chair when I went for the policeman—my father was standing by the fire-side—he was not quiet, he was making a noise and row —I did not see him doing any-thing else—I should have seen it if it had been so—my mother hallooed one two or three times, "Run for a policeman," and I did so—I did not see anybody hold my mother round the body.
COURT. Q. Had your mother had her supper at that time? A. Yes—there was some beer there for mother and father's supper—we had not had our supper—the cloth was on the table—we were going to have some bear and oysters—the oysters were on the table—when my father came down he took the beer and shoved it off the table—my mother did nothing upon that—she was not pleased at it—she said, "That did not hurt me"—he then knocked out the candle—there was only one candle there—then he began his row —I heard him rustling about, and my mother hallooed out two or three times, "Run and fetch the policeman"—she said nothing else.
Q. Recollect yourself; although the prisoner is your father, you must speak the truth; what makes you say he began to make a row when the candle was out; what did you hear? A. Mother said, "Run, he will kill me, he will kill me!"—I did not hear the noise of any blows struck—the table was not turned over—as soon as the row commenced I ran out—it was quite dark—there was no fire—I do not know what the rustling was about—there was no one else there to rustle about, except my father and mother—when I came back again I had a policeman with me—there was no light then—the policeman turned on his lantern—I went to bed two or three hours after that—I had no supper afterwards—there was a light got before I went to bed—my mother lighted a candle—I did not see any mark on her face that sight—I am quite sure about that—I first saw any mark on her face on the Sunday morning—I was not disturbed in the night after this.
WILLIAM HACKETT SHAW ( police-inspector P.) I took the prisoner into
custody on the Sunday night after the deceased's death—he was not sober, and I did not tell him the charge till next morning—I then read it over to him, that it was on suspicion of causing the death of his wife, by violently assaulting her on the night of the 21st of Aug.—he said, "Well, I deny that; I don't recollect striking her at all; but I was very much in liqnor at the time"—the boy William was present at the time the charge was taken, and I said to him, in the prisoner's presence, "Why, you are the boy that came to the station for assistance last Saturday night"—he commenced crying—the prisoner said, "Mind what you are after with that boy"—I repeated the question to the boy, and told him to tell me—he began to cry, and said, "How am I to get my living afterwards?"—I do not recollect that he said anything else.
COURT to MR. GOWAR. Q. You know the condition in which this woman's lungs were, in point of disease; from your inspection, suppose a person so afflicted was very much exasperated and excited by anger, would that be likely to produce hemorrhage? A. It would not, I think, to the extent which I observed—I think the excitement from anger might not the sufficient.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 54.— Confined Three Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

2237. JOHN ANNS was indicted for unlawfully obtaining, by false pretences, 5 s. 2 d., the moneys of the Eastern Counties Railway Company; he was also charged with obtaining money from two other Railway Companies: to all of which he pleaded.
GUILTY. Aged 21.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18470920-2238]
2238. JOHN CUSICK and JAMES CHIDDY were indicted for feloniouely breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Elizabeth Field, at Lambeth, and stealing therein 1 pencil-case, value 18 s. 1 locket, 30 s.; and 1 shirt-stud, 1 s. 6 d.; her goods: 12 spoons, 5 l.; 1 fork, 1 l.; 1 strainer, 15 s.; 1 pepper castor-top, 2 s.; 1 mustard-pot, 2 l.; 1 scarf, 13 s.; and 1 half-sovereign; the property of Ann Wanless: to which
CUSICK pleaded GUILTY .— Transported for Ten Years.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH FIELD. I am a widow, and reside at No. 50, Henry-street, Kennington-lane. On Sunday evening, the 19th of Sept., about twenty minutes to seven o'clock, I left home to go to chapel—I left no one in the house—I saw that it was all safe—I locked up both in front and behind myself—I pulled the street-door after me—it has a spring-lock—I brought away the key—I locked the gate of the garden in front of my house, and brought away the key—all the windows were left fast—I was called out of chapel, and got back to my house about half-past seven—I found the garden gate unfastened—the street door was shut, as I had left it—I opened it with my key, and went in—I could not have told that anybody had got in—the door must have been opened by a key—there were no marks of violence—it was not a latch-key, but a large street-door key—I first went into my front parlour, and missed nothing from there—the tea-caddy was laid on the back—parlour table, with the lock hanging to it—there was nothing but the sugar in it—it had been broken open—nothing was taken from it—the drawers in the back parlour had been opened, and the contents were strewed on the floor—I
went into my own chamber, missed a gold pencil-case, a locket, and one shirt-stud—I had a lodger, named Ann Wanless, who had left on the Saturday evening to go into country—I went into her room, and found there had been a visitor there—her writing-desk had the lock broken, the things were strewed on the table, and some articles of plate were gone—I know these articles of plate—( produced)—they belong to Ann Wanless—I had seen them on the premises on the Saturday on which she felt, and they were gone on the Sunday evening—I can swear to all of them—I should say the property exceeds the value of 5 l.—these are all silver.
FLORES EMMENS. I am single, and live next door to Mrs. Field. On Sunday evening, the 19th of Sept., I remember seeing her go out to chapel—I observed her lock the door and gate—about ten minutes or quarter of an hour after she was gone, I saw two men near her house—I watched them—they stood near our railing, loitered about a little while, then crossed the road, came back again, opened the gate, and went into her house—I do not know how they undid the gate, but I saw them undo the door—they applied some thing to the door, opened it, went in, shut the door after them, and I heard the blinds in the house pulled down—I went for a policeman.
CAROLINE RUSH. I am the wife of John Rush, of No. 15, Henry-street, opposite to Mrs. Field's. On this Sunday evening Miss Emmens' sister came across, and told me something; in consequence of which, I looked out towards Mrs. Field's house, and saw the figures of two people moving in the first floor front room—the blinds were drawn down, the candle was on the opposite side of the room they were white holland blinds, and the candle showed their figures—they were removing things constantly to and fro—I could not see the articles they moved—I saw them moving things from the cheffonier in the room—before the policeman came, I saw two persons came out of the house—it was not light enough for me to observe them, but I could see their figures very well—one was a short stout man, and the other tall and thin—I screamed out when I saw them come out—they heard at and attempted to strike my husband, who came up—they ran away—I ran after them—they got out of my sight—I did not see either of them taken—I afterwards saw one of them in the hands of constable Sewry, he resembled the tall man I had seen—I afterwards saw some skeleton keys lying in Mr. Field's garden, and I found just inside the garden the top of a silver cruet.
Cross-examined. Q. Can you undertake to swear whether there were two or three persons in the room? A. Two—I saw them come out—I was standing on the pavement outside my gate, and I could discern two figures—I should say Chiddy was one of those two persons, by this figure—I cannot swear to the face—I will not swear that he was one of the two.
JOHN SEWRY ( policeman, L 33.) I was on duty in Kennington-lane on this Sunday evening—in consequence of what I heard, I obtained the assistance of another policeman—he went to the back part of Mrs. Field's house, and I went to the front—just before I got to the top of Henry-street I heard a cry of "Stop them, there they go!" and I saw the two prisoners come running up Henry street—Mrs. Rush pointed to the tall one (Chiddy) and desired me to stop him—I stopped him—she said he was one of the two men that were in the house—he said he knew nothing about it—I had laid hold of him at the time—he said, "What do you want to stop me for, I am a respectable man?"—I said, "If you are a respectable man you are my prisoner, and must go to the station"—he asked me what I wanted to take him there for, and resisted very much—I called the assistance of a civilian, who was there, to help me, and took him to the station—before I had him in custody
I saw him throw something away in the shape of a crowbar, or jemmy, I could not exactly see, it was between the lights—he threw it into a garden-gate—after I had lodged him at the station I went back to Mrs. Field's house, and found these five skeleton-keys in her garden—Chiddy must have out at the gate—in the road, I found this top of a winestrainer, a candle, some matches, and these bits of handkerchief—these silver spoons were picked up by another witness, and this life-preserver was given me by a man picked it up over in a garden, in the direction in which I had seen Chiddy throw something—I did not see it found.
Cross-examined. Q. You said he threw something away like a crow bar? A. I would not swear it was a crowbar—I will swear he threw something—the life-preserver was given me next evening—I should have gone to the house that night, but I could not have got in, as the man was in bed—he is not here—Chiddy said that he was in Henry-street making water, and he heard cries of "Murder," and "Stop thief." and that he ran up—when I stopped him, he said he was innocent.
WILLIAM WRSTON ( policeman, L 65.) On the Sunday evening in question I went toward the back of Mrs. Field's house—I heard a cry that the men had got out—I went round to the front and met Cusick, and took him into custody—I searched him, and found on him this silver mustard-pot, seven desert-spoons, part of a wine-strainer, two salt-spoons, one mustard-spoon, a gold pencil-case, a gold locket, a silver shirt-stud, a knife, and several skeleton-keys—this crowbar was picked up and given to me.
ANN WANLESS. I am single, and lodge at Mrs. Field's. I left home on the Saturday before the robbery, and returned on the Wednesday evening—these articles are all mine, except the locket and pencil-case—I left them all safe in my room when I left on Saturday evening.
JOHN SEWRY re-examined. I was before the Magistrate when Chiddy made a statement that was taken down, read over to him, and signed by the Magistrate—this is Mr. Elliott's signature—( read "The prisoner Chiddy says, I was standing in Henry-street making water; I heard the cry of 'Stop thief!' and 'Murder!' I ran up the street, and the policeman laid hold of me; I asked him what he stopped me for, and said I was innocent of it")—the wine-strainer was picked up in the road four or five yards from Mrs. Field's—I saw both the prisoners running up the middle of the road together—one was on one side of the way, and the other on the other—I did not see Chiddy near spot where anything was found.
CHIDDY NOT GUILTY.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18470920-2239]
2239. SARAH BROOKS was indicted for stealing 4 sovereigns, and 2 half-sovereigns, the monies of Frederick Dadswell, from his person, having been before convicted of felony.
FREDERICK DADSWELL. I am shopman to Frederick Price, of Bishopsgate-street. On the 30th of Aug., about twelve o'clock at night, I was in High-street, Borough—the prisoner stopped me, and wished me to go to a house in King-street with her, which I would not do—she would not leave me—after refusing her several times, we went into a public-house, and I gave her a pint of beer, with an understanding that she was to leave me—that was five minutes after I first met her—we came out—I told her to go one way, and I would go the other—she would not, but kept hold of my arm, and followed me—she pulled me into another public-house by force, and called for some gin—I told her she ought not to do it—I paid 5 d. for it—we came out—I told her to have me—we stood still together ten minutes, opposite the Town-hall—I
did not see a policeman—I should not have given her in charge if I had—I went towards the Borough-market—she attempted to pull me into another-public-house—she suddenly pulled me into a dark court, and closed the door—I felt my money in my pocket then—she began unbuttoning my trowsers, saying as I had treated her she would treat me—I felt for my money, missed it, seized her hands, and said she had robbed me—she struggled to get away—she said I had dropped something—I dragged her out, met a policeman at the court door, and gave her in charge—she told the parcel—I had four sovereigns and two half-sovereigns in a piece of newspaper in my right hand trowser's pocket a quarter of a minute before I missed them.
Prisoner. Q. Did you say as you had treated me I must treat you? A. No.
CHARLES M'CARTHY ( policeman, M 252.) On the 30th of Aug. I was on duty in the Borough-market—Dadswell gave the prisoner into my charge—she said she heard something drop in the court—he charged her with stealing few sovereigns and two half-sovereigns—she denied it—I took her to the station.
MARY ANN RANDALL. I search females at the Southwark Police-station—the prisoner was brought to the station—she said she had got no money—I searched her, and found four sovereigns and two half-sovereigns, wrapped up in a piece of paper—she took it from me once, and wished me to keep it, and not say anything about it.
Prisoner. I did not wish you to keep it; I wished to know what it contained.
FREDERICK DADSWELL re-examined. There is no mark on this paper—it was such a piece of newspaper as this—to the best of my belief it is the same.
Prisoner's Defence. If I had known it belonged to the gentleman, I would given it him.
GEORGE WILD ( policeman, M 94.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted Aug., 1845. and confined one year)—I was present at the trial—she is the person.
GUILTY. Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18470920-2240]
2240. HENRY KITCHENER was indicted for stealing 1 pair of scales, value 2 s.; 1 frame and glass, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 cannister, 6 d.; 1 bottle, 6 d.; 1 clock, 5 s.; 1 carpet, 2 s.; and other articles, value 20 l.; the goods of Robert Lewis King.
MARY KING. I am the wife of Robert Lewis King, of Norfolk-street, Southwark Bridge-road. The prisoner is my son, but not my husband's son—my husband and I were tried for felony—it was for keeping goods sent to us to mangle—we were acquitted—we had the goods in the house, but we did not know who they belonged to—before we were acquitted the prisoner came for the key of the house, to take care of the things—when we were acquitted, we went back to the house, found it quite empty, and the key given up to the land-lord—we lost the whole of our household furniture—18 l. or 19 l. would not replace them—I have seen the things produced—I am confident this gown and shawl are mine—the other things are my husband's, and were in the house when we were taken to prison.
Prisoner. Q. On the 23rd of July did not you give me the key to pledge the things and pay the back rent? A. No. you sent a letter to me at Horse-monger-lane, and said if I would allow you to have the key, you would live in my cottage, and take care of it, and pay the rent till I came home.
COURT. Q. How was he to pay the rent? A. Nothing was said about that—if he had pledged things to pay the rent I should have thought nothing about it—he had no authority to pledge things—he come to me on the following Thursday week, and told me he had moved part of the things from the cottage—I asked him what he had moved them for—he said he did not think, it I was acquitted, I should like to come to the same neighbourhood to live.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not ask me, at the police-court, what I had made away with, to pay the back rent, and did not I say, "Mr. King's cost?" A. I know nothing about pledging anything to pay back rent—you said you had moved the things into Lant-street—that was a wrong address.
ROBERT LEWIS KING. I am the husband of the last witness. When we were acquitted we went back to our house, and missed everything we had left there—I went to Lant-street, in search of the prisoner—he was not there—I found him in Billingagate, and gave him in charge—he said at the station he had pawned my clothes, and sold his mother's in Petticoat-lane—he did not say he had any authority to do it—he had none from me.
ALEXANDER HAUGHEY ( policeman, P 188.) I took the prisoner, and told him Mr. King charged him with taking all he had got from his cottage, and asked him where the things were—he told me he had sold them in Petticoat-lane—I asked if he had any idea who to—he said, "No"—I asked him about Mr. King's clothes—he said they were pawned at William's, in the Borough—at the police-court he said he had sold the things to a broker, (he would not tell where,)and part of them at the house where he lived—the Magistrate ordered me to go with him the following day to his lodgings, No. 5, Norfolk-street—we there found a lot of property—he took me to Long-lane, Bermondsey—we there found a great deal there—we have got the greatest part of it back—I produce some duplicates which I found at the prisoner's lodging.
JAMES EDWARD MORGAN. I am a furniture-broker, at 168, Bermondsey-street. About the 5th of Aug. the prisoner came to me, and said a person named Reynolds had sent him, who had seen some furniture which he had to sell, but they were not good enough for him, and asked if I would buy it—I went to No. 5, Norfolk-street, and bought a bedstead, a bed, five chairs, and four boxes, which have since been owned by Mr. King.
ROBERT LEWIS KINGS re-examined. I have been to Mr. Morgan's and seen the articles—they are all my property.
Prisoner. They are not; they were my mother's, and you are not married to her, though you live as man and wife. Witness. They are the property of the house, and were under my care.
SAMUEL ALLMAN. I am a shoemaker. I bought some crockery ware, chins, and glass, to the amount of 7 s. 11 d., at No. 5, Norfolk-street—I occupy the bottom of the house.
MARY KING re-examined. I have seen the things bought by Allman—they are my property.
DAVID GRIMMETT. I am in the employ of Edward Burles, King's-road, Walworth. I produce two pillows and two waistcoats, pledged by a woman, at the latter end of July—I gave a duplicate for them, it is among these produced—Mrs. King has seen them.
MRS. REYNOLDS. I keep a furniture-broker's shop. The prisoner came to me, and said he had some furniture to sell, and asked if I would buy it—I said, "Yes," if it suited me—he said he was not quite ready then, but next morning he would be prepared for me—he came next morning, and said, "I have got the things to dispose of; I am going into the country, and must sell
them to-day"—I went him to Norfolk-street, and saw some things, but did not buy them—I recommended him to go to Mr. Morgan.
Prisoner's Defence. She ordered me to pawn something to pay the back rent, which was done the next day, the 22nd of July; they have been to the landlord; and they know that the rent is paid.
ROBERT LEWIS KING re-examined. He paid 6 s. rent, which he pawned a coat for—the rent was 3 s. a week—the goods are worth 18 l. or 19 l.—he never gave me any money for the goods he sold.
MARY KING re-examined. He never gave me any money—he had as commission from us to sell.
GUILTY. Aged 28.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18470920-2241]
2241. WILLIAM JOLLY and PHŒBE JOLLY , were indicted for stealing, at Christchurch, 12 sovereigns, 1 half-sovereign, 2 half-crowns, 5 shillings, and 1 groat; the moneis of Robert Corlett, in the dwelling-house of James Richard Gale.
ROBERT CORLETT. I am a clerk in the General Post-office, and live in Hatfield-street. Mr. Gale is the landlord—the prisoner lived on the same floor with me. On Sunday afternoon, the 5th of Sept., about half-past five o'clock, I went into my room and found my trunk open—it had been locked before I went out—I missed 12 l. 10 s. in gold, two half-crowns, 3 s., and a four penny piece—I had put some of it in on the Saturday afternoon before—one of the shillings had a hole through it—I had locked up my box, and taken my key with me—I went with two policemen to the prisoner's room and saw then—they gave leave to have their boxes searched—I saw two half-sovereign found, and two half-crowns and five shillings, the same amount of coin as my silver, except the fourpenny piece—one of the shillings had a hole in it, similar to the one I missed—the gold is gone.
William Jolly. Q. Did you describe that shilling to the policeman? Witness. No; I described the coin, but forgot the shilling with the hole in it—there is no other coin which I can identify—the gold was nearly new—I know the half-sovereign was dated 1847, the one found is of the same date—the fourpenny piece was one of King William's—the other people's boxes were not searched.
SARAH GALE. I am wife of James Gale, of Hatfield-street—my husband rents the house, lives in it himself, and lets out apartments—I believe it is is the parish of Christchurch—on Wednesday, the 18th, the prisoners came to lodge there—they passed as man and wife, and said they had just come from Manchester—Corlett lodged there on the Friday before this happened—he sent me for change for a sovereign—I gave it him, and noticed a shilling with a hole in it—on Sunday, the 5th of Sept., about five o'clock in the afternoon, William Jolly came down stairs, he seemed rather confused, and asked me for the bellows—I said I was using them—he after wards came down, I gave them to him, and he went out—he was out about fan hour and a half—I was not out of the house during that time—I opened the door to him—I had not opened the door to any one while he was out, except Mr. Corlett—no stranger came in during that time—there was no other person on that floor but Corlett, the prisoners, and myself—I am quite sure this shilling ( produced) is the one I got.
William Jolly's Defence. The money found on us was our own; I heard several persons enter Corlett's room during the day.
SARAH GALE re-examined. A lady and gentleman came to my house on Saturday night, and took the lower room, but they have not seen the top room—they are very respectable people, and are still there—I swear they
had no opportunity of going up stairs to that floor—I was with then all the time.
JURY. Q. What part of the house do you do you live in? A. The kitchen, which is up stairs—the lady and gentleman live in the room above—they could not go to the room above without my knowing it, because they did not know the way of the house, or who I had got in it—they were hardly out of the room during the day I went for the change.
ROBERT CORLETT re-examined. I came home on the Saturday evening, between eight and nine—I remained down stairs with Mr. and Mrs. Gale, not in the room where I kept my money—I had left that room soon after four—I locked it, had the key with me, and the key of my box—I did not go up to the room till I went to bed—the box was not opened then—the money could not have been taken till Sunday—I slept there that night, and left the trunk safe at a quarter-past ten on Sunday morning—I tried it.
JURY. Q. Had you the key of the room with you on Sunday morning? A. No, I left it in the door for Mrs. Gale to make up the room—the lid of the trunk was not broken, it was unlocked—it did not seem to have been forced—I tried the key of my room to a box in the prisoners' room, in the prisoner's presence, and it fitted it; but I have not seen the key of the prisoners' box.
WILLIAM GREY ( policeman.) I took the prisoners, and found the money in their box—the house is in the parish of Christchurch.
JURY to SARAM GALE. Q. Was the hole in the shilling a drilled hole, or a punched hole? A. It was a little raised on one side—it was at the top of the head—I had received a fortnight's rent from them—14 s. altogether, 4 s. of which was for attendance during her confinement—it was all in shillings—I did not see them with any gold.
WILLIAM JOLLY — GUILTY.
PHŒBE JOLLY — NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470920-2242]
2242. WILLIAM JOLLY and PHŒBE JOLLY were again indicted for stealing 1 crown, 2 half-crowns, 1 shilling, and 1 sixpence; the monies of Mary Ann Carnes.
MARY ANN CARNES. I am a widow, and live at 9, Princes-street, Union-street. On the 11th of July the prisoner Phoebe came to me, told me she was a cap-maker, and wanted part of a bed—I took her to sleep with me—I took her for a single woman—on the Tuesday before she left on the Wednesday, the prisoner William came—they had an interview—they did not say what relationship there was between them, they both break fasted with me that morning—I went out between twelve and one o'clock, leaving them in my room, and nobody in the house but a little girl twelve years old—I was away three quarters of an hour or an hour, came back, and found the prisoners had just dined—they left between seven and eight o'clock, leaving no one in the house but me—he said to her, "Now, my dear, shall I take your box;" he went away taking her box—two hours afterwards I went to my drawers, I found them locked as I had left them, and missed my money which had been in a little brown bag—nobody else had been in the house—I did not see the prisoners again till they were in custody—I had given her warning—I never saw bag or money again—there was nearly 3 l. in it.
William Jolly. Q. Did not a young man lodge in the house? A. Not that night—there was only an old man lodged in the house, he house, he was out all day—I had seen my money in the drawers the night before—they had not been opened since: I had the key in my pocket.
William Jolly's Defence. I came up from the country, and joined my wife, who was living at this house, intending to get furnished lodgings next day; we got lodgings at Mrs. Gale's, in Hatfield-street, not ten minutes walk from the prosecutrix's, who we met frequently in the streets, and she did not say a word about this affair till we were examined the third time at the Police-court on the other charge.
MARY ANN CARNES re-examined. I never saw them after they left, till they were in custody, they might have seen me—I found out that they were in custody by Mrs. Gale coming to know where they lived before—I did not know where they went to.
William Jolly. I said we are going not far from you, into Hatfield-street. Witness. Nothing of the kind passed—if I had met you I should have given you in charge
WILLIAM JOLLY — GUILTY. Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.
PHŒBE JOLLY — NOT GUILTY.

2243. JOHN PARKER was indicted for stealing 1 pair of boots, value 5 s. 6 d.; the goods of Thomas Robert Dutton: and having been before convicted of felony.
FREDRICK HENSTOCK I am shopman to Thomas Robert Dutton, a boot-maker. On Friday evening, the 20th of Sept., I was at the end of the counter, near the window—I could not be seen from the door—I saw the prisoner take a pair of boots from the stand, put them under his coat, and walk out of the shop—I went out, found him standing two shops off, took hold of him, and taxed him with having the boots—he dropped them at my feet, and said, "He gave them me"—he did not point to anybody—I am sure he is the person—he was never out of my sight—these boots are Thomas Robert Dutton's.
JOSEPH RACKSTRAW ( policeman.) I was duty, and saw the prisoner struggling with Henstock—I took him and the boots—these are them ( produced)—going to the station, the prisoner said he did not steal them, some one gave them to him.
JOHN UNDERWOOD ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted, May, 1843, and confined three months)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
