2245. MATILDA BATES was anain indicted for stealing 2 brushes, value 2 s. 6 d.; 2 yards of satin, 3 s.; 1 box of tooth-powder, 1 s.; and 1 bottle of tartaric-acid, 1 s.; the goods of John Spencer, her master.
THOMISINA SPENCER. I am the wife of john Spencer, of Larkhall-rise, Clapham. The prisoner was in my service—I gave her notice to leave on the 22nd of Sept.—I had given her a character for another place that morning—I had no idea that she was dishonest, except in petty things—the same afternoon I went into her bedroom with a friends, who took up her comb-bag—the prisoner was not there, she was down stairs—we found in it two tooth-brushes, and a nail-brush with "15 d." marked on it—the private mark was erased—I could see that it had been there at the time but the mark and price is rubbed off now—I am able to swear they are my husband's—they were never sold—I called the prisoner
up, and said, "Matilda, you have been taking things that do not belong to you, out of the surgery"—she said, "No, I have not"—she afterwards said, "Pray, ma'am, forgive me"—I opened her box, and found some trimming, and some stuff cut into pocket-handkerchiefs, a baby's frock cut into handkerchiefs—a box which had been taken out of a drawer, and then filled with tooth-powder—it was our box and tooth-powder; also a box of pastiles; also a puff-box and powder, which was taken from a glass-case in my dressing-room—I also found a handkerchief of mine under her pillow—she said she had taken them, and prayed of me not to expose her, as I had given her the character—I desired her to undress herself, she refused—I said I must call in a policeman—she said, "Call one in"—she presently asked me if she gave me all she had, if I would call one in—I said I would make on promise—she pulled a bead-purse from under her dress—I also found a packet of violet powder which had been taken out of the surgery—she had lived with me a fortnight—I had a character for honesty with her.
JOHN DEMPSEY ( policeman) I took the prisoner—she said she did not know what possessed her to do such a thing—I received the property from Mrs. Spencer.
GUILTY. Aged 19.— Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18470920-2246]
2246. GEORGE STREAK was indicted for stealing 1 ass, price 1 l. 5 s.; the property of William Edwards.
WILLIAM EDWARDS. I live in Little Manor-street, Clapham. On Friday, the 5th of Aug., I had a donkey turned out on Clapham-common—I saw it safe at nine o'clock in the morning, and missed it about half-past nine—I saw it again about four the same afternoon, in Smithfield—a man named Timson had it there for sale.
EDWARD LAWRENCE. I live at Walworth. On the 6th of Aug., about twelve o'clock in the day, I was in Blackfriars-road—I saw a donkey—a little boy was riding it, and the prisoner was walking by the side of it—the prisoner asked me to buy the donkey—he asked 12 s. for it—I said I had only 8 s.—they went on about forty yards, and then the little boy came runing back, and said I should have it—I bought it for 8 s.—I am sure it was the prisoner who offered me the donkey for sale—I took it to my own home, and put it in harness to see if it would be of any use to me, and it would not, it laid down—I sent Timson to sell it for half a guinea—I afterwards heard something, and the prisoner was given in charge.
WALTER CHARLES DODD. I live at No. 2, Union-street, Walworth. I was with Lawrence when he bought the donkey—I saw the prisoner—he had a boy with him—I did not hear the dealing, but I saw the money paid by Lawrence to the prisoner.
WILLIAM GIBBS ( policeman, V 77.) I apprehended the prisoner—I told him he was charge with stealing a donkey—he told me he did not take the donkey off the common; but if he had been present when the other boy was tried, he would have given the young b—r more than he had got.
Prisoner's Defence. I met the boy on Kennington-common; he asked me to go with him; I went with him to Blackfriars-road, and he sold it there; he said, "It is one of Mr. Edwards's donkeys, but don't say anything about it;" he gave me half the money, and had half himself.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470920-2247]
2247. JAMES LAWSON, BENJAMIN LAWSON, WILLIAM JOHNSON , and JOHN MURPHY , were indicted for stealing 1 purse, value 6 d.; 2 shillings, and 3 sixpences the property of John Rathbone Taylor, from the person of Caroline Taylor; and that Murphy had been before convicted of felony: to which
JAMES LAWSON pleaded GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.
BENJAMIN LAWSON pleaded GUILTY . Aged 13.— Confined Three Months.
WILLIAM JOHNSON pleaded GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Six Months.
MR. PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
CAROLINE TAYLOR. I am the wife of John Rathbone Taylor—I was walking down Blackfriars-road on the 16th of Aug.—I had a purse with me containing shillings and sixpences—one of the sixpences was battered—I went on to the London-road—I was spoken to by Pound—I felt in my pocket, and my purse was gone—this now produced is it—this sixpence is similar to the one I had—the prisoners were not in sight when I was spoken to—I had seen James Lawson several times before in the London-road.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK Q. You do not swear to this sixpence? A. No. nor to Murphy.
WILLIAM POUND. I am a labourer, and live in Duke-street. On the 16th of Aug. I was in the Blackfriars-road, and saw Mrs. Taylor—the two Lawsons were there—James Lawson had a butcher's frock on—I saw Benjamin Lawson attempt to Mrs. Taylor's pocket close by Charlotte-street—she was walking away from Blackfriars-bridge towards the Obelisk—I saw Johnson and Murphy about a quarter of an hour afterwards in conversation together, near the Duke of Clarence, at the corner of the London-road-Mrs. Taylor came up, followed by the two Lawsons—Johnson and Murphy joined the two Lawsons, and they had a conversation together close by Mr. Alder's, the cook's shop—they then went on, and I saw Benjamin Lawson take the purse from Mrs. Taylor's pocket, and they all ran away—Mrs. Taylor had made a stop, and the purse was then taken—the prisoner ran down Gaywood-street—I went another way, round West-square—I found them in St. George's-road—I afterwards took the two Lawsons.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you sure it was Murphy you saw? A. Yes, I have known him for the last twelve or fourteen years—he was close to Benjamin Lawson when he took the purse, and he held his coat up—he was taken the same evening—I collared the two Lawsons—Murphy and Johnson ran back from the other two—I had just left my work, and was coming along Blackfriars-road—I deliver bills for the different theatres, the Surrey and Astley's, and the Victoria—I had left off at four o'clock, and was coming home—I am not supposed to deliver bills every day—I only do so once a week—sometimes I get a job otherways—I was a constable eight years—when I see a person robbed, I am the first to go to take the thief—I do not do a bit of police-work unless 1 see a person robbed—I followed Mrs. Taylor all the way to the London-road, because I was quite satisfied they were going to rob her—I left the police, as I told you once before, because I got a drop too much—I always feel proud of coming as a witness if I see a person robbed-perhaps I may have been a witness fifty times at the Old Bailey and Surrey Sessions, and several other places, out of town and in town—I am not aware what they call me—perhaps the thieves may call me a policeman's cad —I do not know it—I am always the first to give information—perhaps they would take me back into the police if I were to ask them.
DAVID COTTON. I saw all the prisoners that night, between six and half-past six o'clock, by the corner of the Duke of Clarence, in the Circus, leading up from the London-road—I saw Pound take the two Lawsons into custody, and I saw this purse drop from Johnson.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you refer to Murphy as being there? A. Yes—I had not known him before—I am sure it was him—I never saw him till he was taken into custody—that was after the lady had been robbed—Pound took the two Lawsons, and called to Stroud, and said, "Take these other two men"—I saw nothing of the lady—I saw Johnson drop this purse—I did not hear any cry of "Police."
JOHN BAKER ( policeman, L 128.) Pound pointed out Murphy, and I took him into custody—he was not with the other three prisoners when I took him—he was with them when he came out of the St. George's tavern—he had been pointed out to me when he was in the tavern—they came out, and walked about 100 yards, and then Murphy left the other three, and went back, and I took him—I found on him 1 s., a knife, and two penny-pieces—on Johnson I found 4 s., two sixpences, 9 d. in halfpence, and a comb—among the sixpences was this battered one.
Cross-examined. Q. All you know is, that Pound pointed out Murphy and the others being all together? A. Yes, they were close together when they came out of the tavern.
JESSE CANNON ( policeman, L 69.) I produce a certificate of Murphy's former conviction at this court—( read—Convicted the 7th of April, 1845, and confined two years)—I was present at the trial—he is the man.
MURPHY — GUILTY. Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.

2248. CHARLES GIBSON ROGERS was indicted for embezzling 19 s. 8 1/2 d.; the moneys of George Marshall, his master; to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18470920-2249]
2249. JOHN WRIGHT was indicted for stealing 120lbs. weight of lead, value 1 l.; the goods of the South Eastern Railway Company.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM SHAW. I am in the employ of the South Eastern Railway Company. I attend to the goods department at the Bricklayer's Arms station, on the night duty—the goods at this time of year chiefly come up at night—we have two trains come in, one at twelve o'clock, and the Dover train at half-past three o'clock in the morning—they bring market goods; fruit, fish, and meat—the prisoner was in the employ of Mrs. Stansbury, who carries goods from the station in her own carts—they come about two o'clock every morning, and take fruit, and meat—the cart the prisoner was attached to was driven by Neale, the prisoner was guard to it—on the morning of the 2nd of Sept., I particularly noticed two pieces of lead, which had been brought up from Ashford, and were about to be put in the stores—they were the property of the Company—I saw it safe about two o'clock that morning—I afterwards saw the same lead at Aldermanbury—the prisoner and Neale had been at the station before two o'clock that morning—their employment would give them opportunity to take that lead, if they were disposed to do it—they had their fruit in what we term the dock—after I had been told of the discovery of the lead I went after the prisoner—I found him at the east end of the town—I got him to return with me to Aldermanbury—the
lead was produced, and I asked him how he came in possession of it—he said he found it in the yard of the George, Aldermanbury—there was a bag there, which he said belonged to Neale—I have never seen Neale since I found the lead.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. How far is Mrs. Stansburry's yard from the railway station. A. I should say two miles and a half, or it may be three miles—I observed the two pieces of lead at two o'clock in the morning, on the 2nd of Sept.—there was gas burning in the warehouse—I never took it in my hand—I had looked down upon it and noticed it—there were only these two pieces on the premises—there were a number of persons coming backwards and forwards to this particular spot, perhaps fifteen or sixteen—there are only nine porters there at nigh—I do not know how many there are by day—I know nothing about the day duty—I saw this lead safe at two o'clock, and the prisoner was found with it about eleven o'clock in the morning—I did not miss it from the premises till ten o'clock in the morning—we have had great reason to complain of persons pilfering on the premises—I did not offer the prisoner five shillings if he would tell me the person who had taken the lead.
MR. BODKIN. Q. How long had the lead been there after its arrival? A. It came on the 31st of Aug.—my attention was called to it before the morning of the 2nd of Sept., and I had spoken to the men about it—I saw it in the same place on the 2nd of Sept., at two o'clock in the morning, I missed it at ten o'clock, and did not see it again till after the prisoner had been taken, about seven o'clock in the evening—I am quite confident it is the same lead I saw at the station—the prisoner has been in the habit of coming there for about twelve months—during that time we have lost things—this is the lead—it is worth about 20 s.
MR. WILDE. Q. Was Mr. Knowles present when the wagon was loaded? A. He night have been in the office, or on the station—he said in the prisoner's presence that he was quite sure he did not take the lead.
JAMES HILDER. I am managing clerk to Mrs. Stansbury, the carrier, at the George-yard, Aldermanbury. The prisoner was in her employ—he and a man named Neale went with a cart to the Bricklayer's Arms—I cannot say whether the prisoner went with Neale on the morning of the 2nd of Sept.—I was going out of Mrs. Stansbury's yard that morning, and met the prisoner in the street, within two yards of the yard—it was from half-past eleven to twelve o'clock—he had a bag on his back, which appeared to contain something heavy—I said, "Well, John, what have you got there?"—he said, "A parcel a person gave me at the station, to deliver just down here"—I said, "Who gave you the parcel?"—he hesitated, and said, "That is of no consequence"—I said, "What is it you have got?"—I put my hand to the bottom of the bag, and felt it was very weighty—I told him to come to the gateway, and show me what he had got—he came and opened the bag, and I saw two pieces of lead in it—I said, "Here is something very mysterious here, it has the appearance of rogueishness, take it to the office up the yard"—he took it to the office, and I had it weighed—one piece weighed 84lbs., the other 36lbs.—he gave no further account of how he became possessed of it—I said I must make further inquiry into it, and the lead was detained at the office—I did not see the prisoner again from that time till seven o'clock in the evening, when Mr. Shaw brought him—the lead was then produced—the prisoner said nothing particular about it that I took notice of—he had said in the morning, when I asked him a second time, that he found it in Neal's van; and another account that he gave in the evening was, that be
picked it up in the yard, and he even went and showed us a spot in the corner of the yard of the tavern where he said he had picked it up.
Cross-examined. Q. You have stated that he always behaved like an honest man, and that a great deal of valuable property has gone through his hands? A. I have—Neale absconded, and I have never seen him since.
GUILTY. Aged 34.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Six Months
[trialAccount t18470920-2250]
2250. JANE GRIFFITHS was indicted for stealing 2 aprons, value 2 s., and 1 shirt, 3 s.; the goods of Stephen Betts, the younger; and 1 sheet, value 1 s. 6 d., and 1 curtain, 1 s.; the goods of Stephen Betts, the elder, her master.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
MARY BETTS. I am the wife of Stephen Betts, a butcher, at Putney. The prisoner was in my service about three months—on the 31st of Aug., in consequence of some circumstances, I required to search her box—the policeman was sent for; and on her consenting, I went up to her box in her bed-room, and she unlocked it—I found in it two aprons belonging to my son Stephen, and a sheet cut to pieces—I have put it together to see if it would make a sheet—I can swear to it by a B which I put on it myself, and a little dot—I found a long muslin curtain, which was an apron of my daughter's, and I joined it together myself, and made a curtain—it is my own work—I found a night-shirt of my son's in the kitchen table-drawer, which I can swear to by a B that was on it—the letters have been picked out, but I can see that there has been the cotton there—I asked the prisoner how she came by the aprons—she said she had made half-a-dozen aprons for a young man, and he had given her these two—she said the curtain was her own—I said I could swear to it—she said it was hers, and she had more like it—I told her to show me one like it, and she showed me one which was clear muslin—the sheet, she said, she bought of a housemaid at Streatham, where she lived servant—I had never given her these things—she had no right or authority to have them in her box.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What is there on the aprons that enables you to know they belong to you? A. I had a dozen of them, and I am two short out of the dozen—I missed them after I had searched her box—these are exactly like the others, and the person who made them is here—this shirt is torn—it is not an old one—here is a darn that I know it by, down the seam, and here is a B on it.
ANN FINN. I live at Roehampton. I made a dozen aprons for Mr. Betts, jun.—I am certain these are two of them—I know my own needle-work.
CHARLES CANHAM ( policeman. V 58.) I was called in to be present at the searching of the prisoner's box, and these articles were found—she said she had made half-a dozen aprons for a young man named Jones, in Cookapur-street, and he gave her these two—I went to the place she described, but could not find the person—I communicated to her the result of my inquiry, and she made no answer—she said she bought the sheet of a fellow-servant at Streatham—I went there to make inquiries.
(William Roberts, a linen-draper, in Paul-street, Finsbury, the prisoner's uncle, gave her a good character, and engaged to send for her brother to take her to her friends in the country.)
GUILTY. Aged 28.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and Prosecutor.— Judgment Respited.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

2251. JAMES ANDREWS was indicated for embezzling 8 s.; the moneys of John Jones, his master; to which he pleaded

[trialAccount t18470920-2252]
2252. ELIZA EATON and THOMAS FOSTER were indicted for feloniously receiving 22 yards of cloth, value 18 l.; the goods of William Strange, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM STRANGE. I am a tailor, and live at Tunbridge Wells. On the night of the 19th of Aug. I locked up my house and went out, leaving no one at home—I had at that time a quantity of cloth there—I returned the next morning between one and two o'clock, found my house broken open, and missed about 200 yards of cloth, worth between 60 l. and 70 l.—on the 21st Goddard, the constable came to me, and in consequence of what he said I went to the railway-station—a basket was there opened, and I saw my cloth there—it was repacked, and I saw it again in London—this now produced in it—I have some list here, to compare with this cloth—I am perfectly satisfied it is mine.
THOMAS GODDARD. I am a constable of Tunbridge—on the 21st of Aug. I went to the Railway-station, and found there this hamper—I opened it, and found it contained this cloth—I went to Mr. Strange, who came and identified the cloth—the hamper had been packed up by a basket-maker, and I sent for the same man to repack it—it had this direction on it, "G. Chitty, 2, Arnold's Paragon, Francis-street, Newington Butts, London"—after the hamper per was delivered there, I went into the house—I saw Morton and Eaton talking—the hamper was taken into the parlour, and afterwards taken to the station-house in Lock's-fields.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. It was not opened till it was at the station? A. No.
ISAAC HUBBARD. I am porter to Chaplin and Horne—I took this basket to the house where it is directed—Eaton opened the door—I said I had a hamper, addressed to Mr. Chitty, 2, Arnold's Paragon, Francis-street, Newington Butts—I asked whether such a person lived there—she said, "Yes bring it in, it is all right"—I set it in the passage, and demanded 3 s. 1 d., which was paid me—I said I wanted my book signed, and Eaton took it into the room and brought it out signed "J. Chitty"—the direction on the hamper was G. Chitty.
WILLIAM MORTON. ( police-serjeant, P 7.) I went with Hubbard on the 23rd of Aug., to deliver the hamper—I saw it delivered, and heard him ask what he has stated—he asked to have his book signed, and Eaton took it into the front parlour—I looked through the window and saw Foster lying in bed—I saw him write in the book, and when it was brought out again, I saw the signature in it, J. Chitty—I then went in, and saw the two prisoner talking in the front parlour—I asked Eaton where the hamper was that had just come—she made no reply—I asked her to come with me, and I went into the back parlour—I there saw the hamper—I asked her what was in it—she said she did not know—I said, "It contains cloth stolen from Tunbridge Wells, and I shall take you for receiving it knowing it to be stolen—she said she knew nothing about it—I asked where Mr. Chitty resided—she said she did not know, that he had left there six months, and asked her
to take in any parcels that came—I asked if she was landlady of the house—she said "Yes, but she let it all off but that parlour"—I asked her where Chitty lived—she said she did not know—that he traveled the country, and only called there when he came to London—I went into the front parlour, and found in the drawers three letters, addressed to Mr. Chitty—Foster said the drawers did not belong to him nor the letters.
Foster. Q. Who had the key of the drawers? A. Eaton had.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Chitty? A. I do not know that I should—I do not know that I have seen him—I believe a man named Chitty is known to the police—that is not Foster.
JAMES DABSON. I am a constable of Tunbridge Wells—I came to London and saw the hamper delivered—I went immediately afterwards into the room where Foster was in bed—I called to him four or five times—he appeared fast asleep and would not hear anything I said—the book had been signed not half a minute before—I told him to get up, I must take him about the cloth—he said "Cloth, I know nothing about it"—he said his name was not Chitty, it was Foster—I said "Why did you sign the book John Chitty if your name is Foster"—he said Mrs. Eaton the landlady asked him to do it, and he had done it.
Foster. Q. Was it about half a minute after the book was signed that you came in? A. Yes—instantly.
Foster to WILLIAM MORTON. Q. When did you come in? A. Just. afterwards.
Foster. Dabson says I was asleep, and you said I was talking.
Foster's Defence. Respecting those letters that were found in a drawer, locked up, I had been stopping in the house nearly three weeks previously, and Eston had frequently asked me to read and write letters for her; she could not write; she came into the room that morning when this was delivered, and asked me to sign the book, which I did as a kindness; as to knowing anything at all of the robbery, I do not; the policeman admitted that there is a man named Chitty, who is known; and they know very well my name is Foster; had my name been Chitty, this person would have come and said, "Here is a parcel for you, there is 3 s. 1 d. to pay;" but she paid that and took it into her own apartment; it had nothing to do with me; she merely came and asked me to sign the book so and so, for her; I know nothing of the affair; I had a great number of persons to come up, who know Chitty, and that my name is Foster.
EATON — GUILTY. Aged 33.
FOSTER — GUILTY. Aged 30.
Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against Eaton)
[trialAccount t18470920-2253]
2253. ANN BOWLEY was indicted for stealing 6 spoons, value 2 l.; the property of William Kinnaird Jenkins, her master; and SARAH ASHBERRY for feloniously receiving the same, knowing them to be stolen.
WILLIAM KINNAIRD JENKINS. I live in Victoria Villas, Avilla-road, Regent's Park. Bowley was in my service—about the 27th April, I lost six spoons—these two spoons are mine—they have my mark on them.
HENRY COOK. I am a pawnbroker. I produce these two spoons—Bowley offered them to me on the 27th of April—she asked 22 s. for them—seeing they were two different crests I asked whose they were—she said they belonged to her sister—I told her to fetch her sister, and in the mean time I sent a young man to watch her, to see where she went to—she walked
about the road for some time, came back, and said her sister wanted them back again, and she would not pawn them at all—the young man I had sent came in, and said she had not been anywhere, only walking about—she seemed confused, and then said she would fetch her sister—she went out, and not coming for an hour I went with the officer to the address she gave me, No. 3, Smith's-place—I saw her sitting at tea with her sister, the prisoner Ashberry—I gave her in charge—Ashberry said, "Good God, you have done for yourself now; come in, the neighbours will hear of it now."
JAMES CUDDY ( policeman, L 20.) On the 27th of Aug., I was called by Mr. Cook, and went with him to No. 3, Smith's-place, Gibraltar-row—we found Bowley sitting there—Mr. Cook pointed her out as the person who had offered the spoons—I asked her if she wanted to say anything about the two spoons that she had offered—she said, "Let me have them, I will take them home to my master"—I said, "Where does he live?"—she said, "No.9, Avilla-road, Regent's-part, and his name Jenkins"—I took her to the station—I came back to the house, and asked Ashberry if her sister had left any duplicates there—she said, "No"—I saw her give something to Mr. Cook—I asked what it was, and he gave me a duplicate for two spoons—I said to Ashberry, "Have you any more?"—she handed me a duplicate for two spoons, pawned on the 1st July—she then said, "I have given all I have got, you may search if you please"—I said, "I will not take you, as you have a family; come before the Magistrate to-morrow morning"—I went to Mr. Jenkin's, and found three more duplicates for spoons pawned at Mr. Attenborough's—I went the next day to Ashberry's, and asked why she was not in attendance before the Magistrate—she said, "I went after my girl that pawned the two tea-spoons.
Bowley's Defence. My sister did not know that they were my master's property.
BOWLEY — GUILTY. Aged 28.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor. — Confined One Year
ASHBERRY — NOT GUILTY.

2256. JANE EVANS was indicted for stealing 1 bottle, value 3 d.; 1 half-pint of wine, 2 s. 6 d.; 1 shawl, 3 s.; and 1 book, 6 s.; the goods of Thomas Seel, her master.
THOMAS SEEL. I live at Camberwell. The prisoner was in my service. On the 28th of Aug., about six o'clock in the morning, I found that she had left the house, leaving the back door and the front area open—I missed a pair of boots, a bottle of wine, and a shawl—the boots and shawl were found on the prisoner—they are my wife's.
Prisoner. The shawl my mistress gave me—she shook me very much on Saturday—I took the boots for a fortnight's wages, but not with the intention of thieving. Witness. I never heard of any shaking—the prisoner said she had given the bottle of wine away.
JOHN HALL ( policeman, M 194.) I took the prisoner—she admitted before the Magistrate that she gave the wine away, that the shawl was given her, and she took the boots.
GUILTY of stealing the boots. Aged 18.— Confined Four Months.

2257. THOMAS WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 4 s.; the goods of Charles Dean; having been before convicted of felony.
CHARLES DEAN. I keep a shop in Saville-row, Walworth-road. On the 31st of Aug., about two o'clock in the afternoon, I received information, and ran out—I missed a coat, which had been outside my shop—I informed the policemen—the coat has not been found.
CHARLES COUSINS. I am a journeyman butcher, and live in Baker's-row, Walworth. On Tuesday, the 31st of Aug., I saw the prisoner take a coat down from the front of the prosecutor's shop—he folded it up, and ran away with it—there was another man with him—I am quite sure the prisoner took it.
JAMES CASTLETON ( policeman, P 33.) I found the prisoner running, and took him into custody.
WILLIAM COCKERELL ( policeman, L 108.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at this court—( read—Convicted the 21st of Sept., 1846, having been before convicted of felony, and confined nine months)—the prisoner is the man.

2259. FANNY WARREN was indicted for stealing 5lbs. weight of leather, value 7 s.; the goods of Henry Walker Williams; to which she pleaded
GUILTY. Aged 35.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18470920-2260]
2260. THOMAS NURSE was indicted for stealing 1 half-crown, the moneys of William Rowe, from his person.
WILLIAM ROWE. I live in King-street, East-lane, Walworth. On the afternoon of the 19th of Sept. I was in Battersea-fields, in company with George Double and Thomas Ball—I found the prisoner there with a donkey—I bargained with him for a ride for a penny—I had a halfpenny and a half-crown—I gave him the halfpenny into his hand for the half-crown—I took it out of his hand, and gave him the half-crown—he said, "Go on, you shall have your change when you come back, "and he struck the donkey before I got my legs across—he did not follow the donkey—I went a little way down—when I came back, the prisoner was not there, but another man took the donkey—I afterwards saw the prisoner amongst some men and some donkeys—I asked him for my change—he said, "Go on, you d—d liar, you are to have no change; you gave me two halfpence."
GEORGE DOUBLE. I live in Eagle-street—I was with Rowe in Batterses fields—I saw him give the prisoner a halfpenny first, and he said, "The won't do, "and he gave him a half-crown, and the halfpenny fell out of his hand again—the prisoner struck the donkey before he got his leg across, and he said, "Go on, you shall have your change when you come back"—we found the prisoner a few minutes afterwards, and he said that Rowe had only given him two halfpence, and one was a large halfpenny, the other a small one.
THOMAS BELL. I live in Bedford-row—I was with Rowe and Double—Rowe gave the prisoner a halfpenny—he then gave him the half-crown, and the halfpenny fell into his hand again—he said, "Give me my change?"—he said, "Go on, you shall have it when you come back"—he strack the donkey, and it ran away with the boy half-way down the field.
WILLIAM WALKER ( policeman, L 98.) I was on duty last Sunday evening in Battersea-fields—I apprehended the prisoner—I found on him two six-pences, which he gave to another man to get food for his donkey.
Prisoner. I had two sixpences in my pocket; I told the policeman that was all I had got; this boy had given me two halfpence, and I gave them for change for a sixpence.
COURT to WILLIAM ROWE. Q. Since this occurred, has your master made a charge against you? A. Yes, about receiving 1 l. 14 s.—I was examined before the Magistrate about it—this half-crown has nothing to do with that—I meant to let the prisoner have the half-crown, and to give me 2 s. 5 d. change, but the halfpenny fell into his hand—I expected he would give me 2 s. 5 1/2 d.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470920-2261]
2261. HARRIET MOORE was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 1 l. 5 s.; 4 jackets, 1 l. 12 s.; 5 waistcoats, 15 s.; 11 shirts, 1 l. 2 s.; 4 books, 1 l.; 5 handkerchiefs, 12 s. 6 d; 7 blankets, 1 l. 8 s.; 3 counterpanes,10 s.; 4 pairs of trowsers, 1 l. 2 s.; 2 table-cloths, 5 s.; 1 pair of drawers, 2 s.; 1 sheet, 3 s.; 2 pairs of stockings, 2 s.; and 6 yards of flannel, 5 s.; the goods of Peter Tagg, her master.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
PETER TAGG. I am a slop-seller, and live in Tooley-street, Southwark—the prisoner was in my service, on and off, for three of four years. On Sunday, the 22nd of Aug., she was there, and I gave her into custody—I then went to her lodging, I knew where she lived, as she has been with me so long—the policeman went with me—we found thirty-eight duplicates—I have seen some property of mine at the pawnbroker's—they are here—most of them are mine—five or six of the pawnbrokers gave up the goods—my daughter discovered it, and I told the prisoner to put on her bonnet and shawl, and go with me—she said, "For God's sake, Mr. Tagg, "or "master, forgive me"—my daughter told her she heard her walk in the shop, and my second daughter charged her with stealing a pair of drawers—she professed innocence—she then said she was sorry, and begged I would forgive her.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. It was about the drawers that she said she was very sorry? A. And also about a pair of boots and a waist-coat—I went to her house the same day—I knew it was her house—I was in the habit of sending my boy to her—I think I was once at her house, eighteen months ago, and left some message for her.
JOHN HIGHWOOD ( policeman, M 102.) On the 22nd of Aug. I was on duty in Tooley-street—I went to Mr. Tagg's, and took the prisoner into custody—Mr. Tagg charged her with stealing a pair of drawers, and a pair of boots—she said, "Mr. Tagg, do forgive me this time; I will never do the like again"—I took her to the station—I went on the Monday to several pawnbrokers, and I got a great many articles, which have been shown to Mr. Tagg—the prisoner gave her address No. 34, Brunswick-court, Bermondsey—I went there on the Sunday, and saw a man, who called himself Moore—I found there thirty-eight duplicates, and from them I got the articles at the pawnbrokers—the prisoner owned to the drawers and boots, on the Monday, before the Magistrate, but said she knew nothing about the waistcoat; but of the following Monday, the 30th, she acknowledged everything—she said she could not say but what she had pawned the articles—one dress she said was her own—I should think there were forty pledges.
ANN ASLETT. I searched the prisoner at the station—I found on her twenty-two duplicates, a penknife, and a seal—I marked the duplicates, and gave them up.
HENRY COTTAM. I am a pawnbroker. I produce twenty-five articles, twenty-two of which have been claimed by Mr. Tagg—the prisoner pawned them between Nov., 1846, and June, 1847—the policeman has the duplicates which I gave her from time to time.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you seen the prisoner often? A. Very often—I have known her for the last eleven years pawning at my house—here is a coat and a shirt pawned on the 31st of May—I recollect taking them in—I began then to have some doubt about the matter, and I asked her two or three questions—that was the first time I had suspicion—there were one or two pawnings in June, but I did not take them in—these are the things pawned on the 31st of May.
PETER TAGG re-examined. These are mine—this coat was made only a few days before it was lost, for a person who never came for it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you make it? A. No, certainly not—here is a pair of shoes of mine—I did not make them—this flannel waistcoat I cut out—I cut out all the slops—if I have a bespoke order, I have a foreman to cut it—I bought some of these shoes only two days before—one pair was sold, and another pair was missed immediately after.
GUILTY. Aged 38.— Confined One Year.

2263. MARGARET ANDREWS alias M'Carty , was indicted for stealing 48 yards of diaper, value 1 l. 12 s.; the goods of George Bucknall Picken; having been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM MORTON ( police-sergeant P 7.) On the 2nd of Sept., I was walking down East-lane, Walworth—I saw the prisoner with this bundle under her shawl, in an apron—I heard a whistle—the prisoner looked round—when I got within a few yards of her, she threw down this bundle, and I saw this ticket fall from it—I took her—she tried her best to get away from me—I asked her how she got the bundle—she said she met a man going down Newington-causeway, and asked him to stand a drop of gin, and he said he would if she carried this bundle—I stopped her about 100 yards from Mr. Picken's shop, near Newington-causeway—it is a good half mile from there.
Prisoner. I dropped the bundle on account of its weight; I had no reason to run away; I did not know it was a policeman; I thought it was a gentleman catching hold of me.
ELIZABETH BARRETT. I live in Ann's-place, East-lane. I was in East-lane on the 2nd of Sept., and saw the prisoner throw down this bundle—I took it up—I saw the officer go after her—he had a box with him—she tried to get away from him—I took it from him, or he would have had to throw it down—she tore her shawl in trying to get away.
GEORGE BUCKNALL PICKEN. I live in Crosby-row, Walworth. This diaper in this bundle is mine—it has my private mark on it—it is worth about 30 s. or 2 l.—this ticket is mine—where the prisoner was stopped is about 100 yards from my shop.
Prisoner's Defence. I was not aware it was stolen; a man asked me to carry it; I do not know his name.
ELIZABETH BARRETT re-examined. The prisoner turned several times before she saw the officer—when she saw him she threw it down.
RICHARD DAVIS ( policeman, P 55.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted Aug., 1846, and confined fourteen days)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY. * Aged 18.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18470920-2264]
2264. ELIZABETH TAYLOR was indicted for stealing 1 pair of shoes, value 4 s.; the goods of James Dawson.
JOSEPH RAKESTRAW ( policeman, L 120.) On the evening of the 15th of Sept., I found the prisoner in Cross-street, Walworth—she was intoxicated—I took her to the station, and found on her two pairs of shoes and a pair of boots—when she was sober I asked her for an account of them—she said the boots belonged to her, and one pair of the shoes—I asked her what she could
say about the third pair—she said she was not aware she had a third pair—she did not give me her true address—I have found it out since.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. Did not she say No. 1, Salisburry-crescent, Kent-road? A. Yes, and I believe it is called Salisbury-cottages—one is opposite to the other—it is in Lock's-fields, close by the Kent-road—I saw the prisoner about seven o'clock in the evening—she did not tell me where she bought them.
FRANCES WIRE. I am shopwoman to Mr. James Dawson—he keeps a shoe-warehouse in Newington-causeway. The prisoner brought a pair of shoes to be repaired, and she came on the following Wednesday to fetch them—she bought a pair of boots, and paid for them—this pair of shoes, produced, came out of our shop—we have sold a great many pairs, but not of this make—the prisoner sat with her back to the place where these shoes were—she was sober—the boots were 3 s. 6 d. a pair, and the shoes 4 s.—I am sure these shoes were there in the morning.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe she had dealt at your shop for some time? A. I believe so—I believe her face is known there, and her foot also—I can swear these shoes are my master's—they are a particular made shoe—there are the marks of the size on them—there was no other pair of this size, of this particular make—I cannot tell how many pairs of shoes were in the shop—there were others of this size but not of this material—it is patent seal—I dare say it was about three o'clock when she came—we were very busy; there were many persons in the shop—there was one shopman and one shopwoman besides me; they are not here, they sell shoes as I do—I did not miss these shoes till my attention was called to them by the policeman—I served the prisoner—she paid 3 s. 6 d. for the boots, and 6 d. for the shoes being mended, and went away—I had seen her on the Saturday previous when she brought the shoes.
COURT. Q. As you were a good deal occupied, could you say positively she was not the worse for loquor? A. From her manner of speaking I should say she was not.
COURT to JOSEPH RAKESTRAW. Q. She was too much intoxicated to give any account to you? A. Yes; that was about seven o'clock or a little after—there was no one with her—she was in a pie-shop—the girl was taking some pains with her—when the master came home she flew at him and scratched his face, that brought me in contact with her—the master got her out and brought the shoes to me—the prisoner said they were hers, and she had some money—I took her because she had this property and money—the shoes that were mended, she has on now I believe—these new shoes and boots she had in her hand.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470920-2265]
2265. PETER TAYLOR. THOMAS HEARN , and JOHN ALLEN , were indicted for stealing 20 lbs. weight of lead, value 5 s.; the goods of Edward Herring.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
EDWIN BAKER. I am manager to Mr. Edward Herring, of Trinity-street, St. Mary, Newington. He is a manufacturer of salphate of quinine—he has certain vessels in his manufactory lined with lead, which requires repairing from time to time—the prisoners were employed on the premises a few days ago—they were employed on Thursday the 9th, and of Friday the 10th of Sept.—I gave some directions to Smart and to Lomas—the prisoners left the premises about twenty minutes after five o'clock on Friday evening—I
followed them to the corner of Blackman-street—Trinity-street runs is to Blackman-street—Allen them remained behind with another plumber, and Taylor and Hearn crossed over to Suffolk-street—I should followed them—Hearn left Taylor and went along Suffolk-street, and Taylor commenced running up Blackman-street—I followed Taylor up a stable-yard, and in the yard I saw him take two pieces of lead out of his waistcoat-pocket, and throw them into a window, which was partly open; I laid hold of him—he had them one piece of lead in his hand—I took possession of that, and afterwards got the two pieces of lead from the window—there were about 20 bls. weight of it—it belonged to Mr. Edward Herring—it had been on his premises.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long had Hearn and Allen been employed there? A. Allen only the day previous, Hearn several times for about two months—there was nothing found on Hearn, or Allen, or at their lodging.
Cross-examined by MR. CAARTEEN. Q. Has Mr. Herring any partner? A. No—I have been there about eight months—it is called the "British and Foreign Alkali Company"—Mr. Herring centres in himself the whole of that body—he has told me so—I reside on the premises—Mr. Herring resides at Wandsworth—he attends to the business himself, as I do—I have never seen any partner but him.
JAMES MOORE SMART. I took at Mr. Herring's manufactory. On Friday evening, the 10th of Sept., I watched the three prisoners—I went down stairs about ten minutes before five, and about five minutes before five o'clock Hearn came down—he took something off the right side of the bench in the plumbers' workshop, which appeared to be solder—he doubled it up, and put it into his left waistcoat pocket—he went away, and returned a second time—he bent up some piping, or some sheet lead, I cannot swear which, and secreted that about his person—he went away, and come back the third time—he than beat up some sheet lead, and placed it under his waistcoat—while he was so doing Taylor came down—he beat up three pieces of sheet lead, and placed them under his waistcoat—then Allen came down—he beat up two pieces of sheet lead, and placed them under his waistcoat—they then went up stairs—I went up as quick as I could, and ordered the people in the warehouse to shut the gates, but the prisoners were gone out—I and Bake followed Taylor—I saw him taken in the yard by Baker—I saw Baker pick up one piece of lead—I saw Taylor give Baker one piece, and one piece to Lomas—of course it was lead taken from my master's premises—he went to no other place, because Mr. Baker was after him—I took Allen; he said he knew nothing of it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where were you? A. In the wine-cellar, about twenty yards from the workshop—it is under the warehouse, but in a line with the plumbers' shop—I looked through the open—work of the door—it is a kind of grating—there is about two inches space between each bar—I saw Hearn take something that seemed to he solder, but I cannot say what—Taylor come down while Hearn was down the third time—he beat up three pieces of lead, and placed them under his waistcoat—the prisoners were all together before they went away, but they came in one at a time—Allen was taken the same evening at his own door—no property was found on him, or at his house.
COURT. Q. Did you see Allen do anything? A. Yes, beat up two pieces of lead, and place them under his waistcoat—Hearn was in there three separate times, and the last time he was in, Taylor came, and directly afterwards Allen came in—they could see one another take it.
Cross-examined by MR. CAARTEEN. Q. Who came first into the shop? A. Hearn—I do not knew where the other two prisoners were at the time Hearn took what I considered to be solder—Hearn then left, and came again, and beat up something—I cannot say whether it was piping or sheet lead—neither of the other prisoners were there then—Hearn went away, and came again the third time, and beat up some sheet lead—while he was there Taylor and Allen came—the place is about ten feet long, and eight feet, wide—the man had done work at that time—the prisoners were as near together as they could be—the sheet lead is on the side of the workshop, they were all three at that lead.
JAMES LOMAS. I am in Mr. Herring's service. On Friday afternoon, the 10th of Sept., Mr. Baker told me to watch—I watched, and saw Hearn take two pieces of leaden pipe, about nine inches long—he placed them about his body, and went away—he came back again, and took a piece of sheet lead, he beat it together, and put that about his body, under his waistcoat—he went out, and came in the third time, took some more lead, beat it together, and placed that about his body—Taylor then came in, beat up three pieces of lead, and placed it about his body—I did not see Allen come in, for I left Hearn and Taylor in the room when I went up into the warehouse to give Information to a man to go for a policemen—I then went and watched again—I saw the three prisoners together in the room—they were standing together, and feeling one another down, to see whether they would be noticed in going out—they than came up stairs, and went out—I followed them to the bottom of Trinity-street—they crossed, and Taylor turned towards St. George's Church—I saw Baker run after him—I followed him—he turned down a stable-yard, and there threw away some lead.
CHARLES WALKER ( police-sergeant, M 18.) On Friday, the 10th of Sept, Taylor was given into my charge, and this lead was brought to the station by Mr. Baker—Taylor said the foremen give him the lead to take to the shop.
JOSHUA GOLDIN ( policeman M 87.) I took Hearn that evening in Bedfordbury—I told him what it was for; he said he knew nothing at all of it—I told him I took on a charge of stealing lead from Mr. Herring's premises, where he had been working that day—he said he knew nothing about stealing lead, but he knew there was something up when he saw them following them.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
TAYLOR — GUILTY. Aged 23.— Confined Three Months.
HEARN — GUILTY. Aged 27.
ALLEN — GUILTY. Aged 29.
Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant

2267. ROBERT SALMON was indicted for stealing 112lbs. weight of lead, value 2 l. 10 s.; the goods of Richard Sterry, his master; and AARON PAYNE for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD STERRY. I am an oilman, and live in High-street, Borough; my son and two others are partners with me. I had some lead on the roof of my own private house at Croydon—I had a new roof put on that house—the old lead had been removed—I sold it to Warner and Sons—Salmon was a carman in our service—he was sent to Croydon for the purpose of removing the lead, and he was ordered to call at our place in the Borough—he would have no business in Bermondsey-street with the cart on the 31st of Aug.—it is not in the direction from Croydon to my house in the Borough, or to Mr. Warner's—this lead worth 17 s. a cwt.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. I suppose you do not know where Salmon lives? A. No.; I never knew where he lived—I understood he was in the service of Chaplin and Horne, before he came to us—I do not think we had a character with him—he was employed as a temporary carman while our man was ill—he drove the wagon about a month.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. Who cut the lead at your country house? A. It was cut by a labourer—I sent a person to the police-office to identify some lead—I was there at the time—he was examined before the Magistrate—he is not a witness in this case—he answered the questions put by the Magistrate, but his testimony amounted to nothing.
GEORGE ADAMS ( policeman 156 M.) On the 31st of Aug., between five and six o'clock in the afternoon, I was in Bermondsey-street, and met Salmon driving a wagon—he was coming in a direction from Payne's house—he asked me to drink, and I drank with him—he asked me to mind his wagon—I did so, and he went away for nearly ten minutes—he then came and turned his horses round, and went back, past Payne's shop—in Salmon's absence I had looked into his wagon, and found it contained lead—I received some information, and went to Payne's shop—I asked him whether he had bought any lead—he said no, he had not—Salmon was passing the door at the time, and I said to Payne, "Your bought some lead of that man"—he said yes, he had, but he had not paid him for it—it then left Payne, and went and overtook Salmon at the corner of Tooley-street—I told him he had been selling some lead to Mr. Payne—he said he had not—I said he had, for Mr. Payne had told me so—he said he hoped I would say nothing about it—he produced 5 s., and begged of me to say nothing about it—I threw the five shillings on the ground, and Salmon got into his eat and drove away—I knew him very well, and where he worked—I then returned to Payne's shop, and told him I should go and inform Mr. Sterry of the circumstance—he followed me out of the shop, and begged me not to do it, and offered me half a sovereign—he followed me to Snows'-fields, urging me to receive the money and say noting about it—I did not take the money.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How long have you known Salmon! A. I think nearly three years—he lives in Cherry-garden-street, Dockhead, or somewhere near there—that is below where I saw him—I did not see his wife or children in the cart that day—the cart was coming back towards High-street when I first saw it, as if he had been further down—we had a pot of beer together before he left me—I should say it would take him half an hour to go from where I met him, to his own house—I do not know is whose service he was—I have seen him repeatedly driving a van—the cart appeared to have a full load.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. What time did you go to Payne's!
A. About six o'clock—I did not search Payne's house; Wilde searched it—it was rather an oversight of mine that I did not search the house at six o'clock—I have been about three years in the police—when he offered me 10 s. to compromise a felony, I neither searched his house nor took him into custody—there is a marine-store dealer's next to Payne's; I went in there first—I did not address myself to that store-dealer, I came out again—there is a gateway next to Payne's, it dose not project at all; the front of it is in the same line with the front of his house—I received information, and went to Payne's—the person who gave me the information is not here—he was summoned before the Magistrate, but was not called.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. From the time Salmon left the cart till he came back, could he have gone to his own house? A. No, it was impossible—he could have gone to Payne's after he had left me—he was moving the lead from the front of the cart to the back—I did not search Payne's house, but he admitted the lead was there—he said Salmon had left lead there, but he had not paid him for it—that was said before the half-sovereign was offered.
THOMAS ROGERS. I keep a shop in the archway across Bermondsey-street, On the 31st of Aug. I saw a cart in Bermondsey-street, opposite Payne's door—I saw Salmon take something out of the cart into Payne's marine store-shop—I could not tell what it was, it was rather heavy—he carried it in his arms, open—I was about thirty yards from it—he took it from the hind part of the cart.
COURT. Q. Was it open? A. Yes—I could not tell what it was—it was a light colour—it appeared to be heavy—this is my writing to this deposition—I sthink it might be wrapped up in something—I think it was wrapped up with something, because it was of a light appearance.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Was the railway between you and the cart? A. I was right under the railway—from the way in which he carried it, I should say it was heavy—he carried it openly in his arms—I was examined three or four days afterwards—I can recollect every circumstance for a month as well as for a week—it was between five and six o'clock—it was not raining—I live under the arch—I was in front of my own house—Payne lives on the opposite side to me—the cart was between us—I could see him leave the cart and go into the house—I had never seen the cart before that day—I did not see it pass down with some children in it—I keep a general shop.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. How long elapsed between the time you saw this and the first examination before the Magistrate? A. It happened on Tuesday, and I think on the Monday following I went to the office—I was up twice—I was not examined till the 14th of Sept.—a fortnight elapsed before I was examined, but I was at the office four days before the policeman Adams came to me.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. When did you first mention this circumstance? A The next day—when I heard what had happened, I told the policeman—I saw the there—I saw Salmon sideways as he went in.
GEORGE NICHOL ( policeman M 128.) I was on duty in Snows'-fields on the 31st of Aug.—I saw Adams talking to Payne—I might be two or three yards from them—I saw Payne put his hand into his pocket, take it out, and hold it towards Adams—I heard Adams say, "No, I will not take it"—I heard Payne say, "I hope you will not say anything about it then."
GEORGE WYLD ( policeman M 94.) On the night of the 31st of Aug. I searched Payne's shop—I found this small piece of lead in small tub, like a butter-tub. under the counter—here is about 7lbs. of it—it is what they call cast lead.
WILLIAM SAMPSON. I am a labourer, and live at Croydon. I removed a quantity of old lead from Mr. Sterry's house there, about the 30th or 31st of Aug.—this piece looks like the same sort of lead—it was taken away in three loads—Salmon took one load on the 31st of Aug.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. The lead was generally in large pieces, was it not? A. Some of it was—there were pieces from 1lb. to 1cwt.—it is cast lead, not generally used, not in one house in thirty.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Can you tell which was the heaviest load? A. I suppose the last load—it was the largest in bulk—the first was lighter, the second heavier, and the third the heaviest.
THOMAS HURST. I am in the service of Mr. Warner, of Cripplegate. I received three loads of cast lead from Salmon in Aug.—the last load was on the 31st of Aug., at past seven o'clock in the evening—I told him I thought he was very late—he said he had made all the haste he could—I said, "Do you request to have it weighed to-night?"—he said, "Yes"—I said, "Well, we will weigh it"—the weight of the first load was 39cwt. 3qrs., the second 31cwt. 3qrs. 26lbs., and the third 27cwt. 2qrs. 21lbs.—the second lcad was 8cwt. lighter than the first, and was 4cwt. heavier than the third.
MR. PARRY to GEORGE WYLD. Q. Do not you know that Payne has as one or two occasions given information of parties who wanted to sell his stolen goods? A. I believe that is the case—I have been to his shop several times when anything has been missing, and he has always told me that if anything happened he would let me know momentarily.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470920-2268]
2268. CHARLES DOUGLASS was indicted for stealing 1 breast-pin, value 10 s.; 2 brooches, 2 d.; I sovereign; and 1 half-sovereign; the property of Joseph Brown; having been before convicted of felony.
JOSEPH BROWN. I am pot-boy at the Prince's Head public-house, a Richmond. I had a sovereign, a half-sovereign, and a gold pin, on the 20th of Aug.—on Saturday night, at six o'clock, the window of my room was safe, and everything all close—on Sunday, between twelve and one o'clock, I went to get my clean things in my room up stairs, and saw the window was open, and one pane of glass was broken—I went into the room, and found my box was broken open—I looked into it, and found a sovereign and a half had been taken out, and a gold pin and two brooches—when I had done my business I went to look for a policeman, and told him what I had lost—I told him had suspicion of the prisoner—when I came back the prisoner was in the taproom—the officer questioned him where he was the night before—he said he was at the Waterman's Arms till a quarter to eleven o'clock, and then he went to the Queen's Head, and was there from a quarter to eleven till a quarter is twelve on Saturday night, and while he was there he saw the pin offered for sale.
Prisoner. Q. Did you say you saw me there? A. No, I went to inquire, and they said you were not there.
HENRY MAGNUS. I keep a clothes-shop at Richmond. On Sunday morning, the 22nd of Aug., about twelve o'clock, the prisoner came to me to buy a coat—I sold him one for 5 s.
GEORGE GOODWIN. I keep a beer-shop. The prisoner was there on this Saturday night from seven o'clock till a quarter before ten—he asked me for credit for a pint of beer, and told me he had no money—he came the next morning and had some tea and bread and butter, which came to 6 d.—he went
out again, and came to dinner—he had as much as came to 9 d.—he paid me about half-a-crown in all—it was between one and two o'clock.
Prisoner. My dinner came to 7 d.; I had no beer. Witness. You had a pint of beer, which made it 9 d.
STEPHEM HAYNES. I keep a public-house. The prisoner was not at my house from eleven to twelve o'clock on that Saturday night, to my knowledge.
Prisoner. I came into your tap-room at ten minutes before eleven o'clock, and staid till half-past eleven.
PHILIP PAGE. I saw the prisoner in front of the Prince's Head at ten minutes before eleven o'clock on that Saturday night.
WILLIAM SELBY EDMID ( policeman V 32.) I went to the Prince's Head on the Sunday, and examined the premises—the prisoner came in while I was there—I asked him where he was the night before—he said he was at the Waterman's Arms till a quarter before eleven o'clock, and then he went to the Queen's Head, and stopped till a quarter to twelve—I asked if he had seen anybody that afternoon offering jewellery for sale—he said, "No"—he then said he had heard of it, while he was at the Queen's Head, that two persons came in and pulled something out of their pockets, and said they should be able to get something for it—he described it as a shirt-pin—he said, after he was at the Queen's Head he went home, and slept in his father's cellar—I searched there, and found this iron tool, which exactly corresponds with the marks on the window—here is the window-frame( produced)—this tool exactly fits the marks on it—I believe this tool opened the window.
Prisoner. He picked up this tool out of a dozen more; it is what we use for fixing timbers on the saw-pit. Witness. There was not another like it—this was lying on the top of a tub in the cellar—the others were all pointed, this is not.
JOSEPH BROWN re-examined. Q. How far is the Queen's Head from you? A. About a minute's walk, hardly that—my room is over the stable.
Prisoner's Defence. The gates are left open for anybody; I was never near the house; I had the money saved up to buy me a jacket; I was going to play a cricket-match with Mr. Goodwin.
GEORGE GOODWIN. Yes—you paid me half-a-crown a week before that, to play.
JURY to W. S. EDMID. Q. Did you find any money on the prisoner? A. No—he told me he had no more money, or he should have bought the pin.
STEPHEN CREED ( policeman V 200.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at this Court—( read—Convicted 30th Jan. 1843, and confined six months)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY. Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.

2270. MARY BROWN and MARY ANN JOHNSON were indicted for unlawfully attempting to obtain money by false pretences.
NOT GUILTY.
In the case of
MARY ANN HUNT , convicted at the last session of WILFUL MURDER,
The prisoner was placed at the bar on Saturday, Sept. 25th, before Mr. Baron Platt; and being asked whether she had anything to say in stay of execution, pleaded that she was pregnant. A JURY OF MATRONS was accordingly summoned, who, after having retired and examined the prisoner, found that she was not quick with child. The learned JUDGE stated that the law must take its course.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, OCTOBER 25 TH, 1847.
